 * ❓ Q4: What happens if DataStore.edit throws an IOException?
 *    A: The transaction is NOT committed (atomic). Collect errors via
 *       dataStore.data.catch { }. Unlike SharedPreferences, no silent corruption.
 *
 * ❓ Q5: How do you handle large datasets in Room efficiently?
 *    A: Use Paging 3 library with PagingSource from Room.
 *       Room provides built-in PagingSource support:
 *       @Query("SELECT * FROM articles") fun pagingSource(): PagingSource<Int, ArticleEntity>
 */

06 Networking

06_networking.kt · Kotlin reference

/**
 * ============================================================================
 * 06 — NETWORKING
 * Google SDE-2 Interview Prep
 * ============================================================================
 *
 * Topics Covered:
 *   1. Retrofit — Setup, Interceptors, Error Handling
 *   2. OkHttp — Client Configuration, Interceptors, Caching

 *   7. Network Connectivity Monitoring
 *   8. WebSocket / Server-Sent Events
 *   9. RemoteMediator Summary (full impl → 05_data_persistence.kt)
 *  10. API Design Best Practices
 *
 * ============================================================================

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 1. RETROFIT — SETUP & API DEFINITION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Retrofit = type-safe HTTP client built on top of OkHttp.
 * Converts HTTP API into a Kotlin interface.
 */

interface ArticleApiService {

    @GET("articles")
    suspend fun getArticles(
        @Query("page") page: Int,
        @Query("limit") limit: Int = 20,
        @Query("sort") sort: String = "published_at"
    ): Response<ArticleListResponse>

    @GET("articles/{id}")
    suspend fun getArticle(
        @Path("id") articleId: String
    ): Response<ArticleResponse>

    @POST("articles")
    suspend fun createArticle(
        @Body request: CreateArticleRequest
    ): Response<ArticleResponse>

    @PUT("articles/{id}")
    suspend fun updateArticle(
        @Path("id") articleId: String,
        @Body request: UpdateArticleRequest
    ): Response<ArticleResponse>

    @DELETE("articles/{id}")
    suspend fun deleteArticle(
        @Path("id") articleId: String
    ): Response<Unit>

    @Multipart
    @POST("articles/{id}/image")
    suspend fun uploadImage(
        @Path("id") articleId: String,
        @Part image: MultipartBody.Part
    ): Response<ImageUploadResponse>

    @Headers("Cache-Control: max-age=600")
    @GET("articles/trending")
    suspend fun getTrending(): Response<ArticleListResponse>
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 2. OKHTTP — CLIENT CONFIGURATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * OkHttp handles the actual HTTP communication.
 * Configure timeouts, interceptors, caching, and security.
 */

fun provideOkHttpClient(
    authInterceptor: AuthInterceptor,
    loggingInterceptor: HttpLoggingInterceptor,
    cache: Cache
): OkHttpClient {
    return OkHttpClient.Builder()
        .connectTimeout(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
        .readTimeout(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
        .writeTimeout(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
        .cache(cache)
        // Application interceptors — called once, see final request/response
        .addInterceptor(authInterceptor)
        .addInterceptor(loggingInterceptor)
        // Network interceptors — called for every network request (including redirects)
        .addNetworkInterceptor(CacheControlInterceptor())
        // Connection pool for HTTP/2 multiplexing
        .connectionPool(ConnectionPool(5, 5, TimeUnit.MINUTES))
        // Retry on connection failure
        .retryOnConnectionFailure(true)
        .build()
}

fun provideCache(context: Context): Cache {
    val cacheSize = 50L * 1024 * 1024  // 50 MB
    return Cache(File(context.cacheDir, "http_cache"), cacheSize)
}

/**
 * Interceptor Types:
 *
 * Application Interceptor:
 *   - Called once per request
 *   - Can short-circuit (return cached response without network)
 *   - Can retry and make multiple calls
 *   - Sees the original request (before OkHttp modifications)
 *
 * Network Interceptor:
 *   - Called for every network request (including redirects)
 *   - Can observe data as it flows over the network
 *   - Access to the Connection object (HTTP/2, etc.)
 *   - Sees the request after OkHttp modifications
 */

// Auth interceptor — adds token to every request
class AuthInterceptor(
    private val tokenProvider: TokenProvider
) : Interceptor {
    override fun intercept(chain: Interceptor.Chain): Response {
        val token = tokenProvider.getAccessToken()
        val request = chain.request().newBuilder().apply {
            token?.let { addHeader("Authorization", "Bearer $it") }
            addHeader("Accept", "application/json")
            addHeader("X-Platform", "Android")
            addHeader("X-App-Version", BuildConfig.VERSION_NAME)
        }.build()

        val response = chain.proceed(request)

        // Handle 401 — token expired, try refresh
        if (response.code == 401) {
            response.close()
            val newToken = tokenProvider.refreshToken()
                ?: throw AuthenticationException("Token refresh failed")

            val newRequest = chain.request().newBuilder()
                .header("Authorization", "Bearer $newToken")
                .build()
            return chain.proceed(newRequest)
        }

        return response
    }
}

// Cache control interceptor — modify caching behavior
class CacheControlInterceptor : Interceptor {
    override fun intercept(chain: Interceptor.Chain): Response {
        val response = chain.proceed(chain.request())
        val cacheControl = CacheControl.Builder()
            .maxAge(10, TimeUnit.MINUTES)
            .build()
        return response.newBuilder()
            .header("Cache-Control", cacheControl.toString())
            .removeHeader("Pragma")
            .build()
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 3. SERIALIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Options:
 *   Kotlin Serialization → compile-time, multiplatform, no reflection
 *   Moshi                → efficient, Kotlin-friendly, code-gen or reflection
 *   Gson                 → legacy, reflection-based, slower
 *
 * 💡 Google recommendation: Kotlin Serialization for new projects.
 */

// Kotlin Serialization
@Serializable
data class ArticleResponse(
    val id: String,
    val title: String,
    val content: String,
    @SerialName("author_name") val authorName: String,
    @SerialName("published_at") val publishedAt: Long,
    val tags: List<String> = emptyList(),
    @SerialName("read_time_minutes") val readTimeMinutes: Int? = null
)

@Serializable
data class ArticleListResponse(
    val articles: List<ArticleResponse>,
    @SerialName("total_count") val totalCount: Int,
    @SerialName("has_more") val hasMore: Boolean
)

// Retrofit setup with Kotlin Serialization
fun provideRetrofit(okHttpClient: OkHttpClient): Retrofit {
    val json = Json {
        ignoreUnknownKeys = true   // Don't crash on extra fields
        isLenient = true           // Accept non-strict JSON
        coerceInputValues = true   // Use default values for null on non-nullable fields
        encodeDefaults = false     // Don't send default values in requests
    }

    return Retrofit.Builder()
        .baseUrl("https://api.example.com/v1/")
        .client(okHttpClient)
        .addConverterFactory(json.asConverterFactory("application/json".toMediaType()))
        .build()
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 4. NETWORK LAYER ARCHITECTURE
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Clean network layer pattern:
 *   API Service → Remote Data Source → Repository → ViewModel
 *
 * Remote Data Source handles:
 *   - Calling the API
 *   - Mapping DTO → Domain model
 *   - Error handling and wrapping
 */

// Sealed class for network results
sealed interface NetworkResult<out T> {
    data class Success<T>(val data: T) : NetworkResult<T>
    data class Error(val code: Int, val message: String) : NetworkResult<Nothing>
    data class Exception(val throwable: Throwable) : NetworkResult<Nothing>
}

// Safe API call wrapper
suspend fun <T> safeApiCall(
    apiCall: suspend () -> Response<T>
): NetworkResult<T> {
    return try {
        val response = apiCall()
        if (response.isSuccessful) {
            val body = response.body()
            if (body != null) {
                NetworkResult.Success(body)
            } else {
                NetworkResult.Error(response.code(), "Empty response body")
            }
        } else {
            val errorBody = response.errorBody()?.string()
            NetworkResult.Error(response.code(), errorBody ?: "Unknown error")
        }
    } catch (e: IOException) {
        NetworkResult.Exception(e)  // Network error
    } catch (e: HttpException) {
        NetworkResult.Error(e.code(), e.message())
    } catch (e: SerializationException) {
        NetworkResult.Exception(e)  // Parsing error
    }
}

// Remote Data Source
class ArticleRemoteDataSource(
    private val api: ArticleApiService
) {
    suspend fun getArticles(page: Int): NetworkResult<List<Article>> {
        return when (val result = safeApiCall { api.getArticles(page) }) {
            is NetworkResult.Success -> {
                val articles = result.data.articles.map { it.toDomain() }
                NetworkResult.Success(articles)
            }
            is NetworkResult.Error -> result
            is NetworkResult.Exception -> result
        }
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 5. CACHING STRATEGIES
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Caching levels:
 *
 * 1. OkHttp HTTP Cache (disk):
 *    - Respects Cache-Control headers
 *    - Automatic ETag / If-Modified-Since
 *    - Good for static resources (images, configs)
 *
 * 2. In-Memory Cache (app-level):
 *    - Fast access, no serialization
 *    - Lost on process death
 *    - Good for frequently accessed, computed data
 *
 * 3. Database Cache (Room):
 *    - Survives process death
 *    - Single source of truth (offline-first)
 *    - Reactive queries via Flow
 *
 * Offline-First Pattern (Google recommended):
 *    UI → Room (SSOT) → Repository → API (sync mechanism)
 */

// In-memory cache with expiration
class InMemoryCache<K, V>(
    private val maxSize: Int = 100,
    private val expirationMs: Long = 5 * 60 * 1000  // 5 minutes
) {
    private val cache = LinkedHashMap<K, CacheEntry<V>>(maxSize, 0.75f, true)

    data class CacheEntry<V>(val value: V, val timestamp: Long)

    @Synchronized
    fun get(key: K): V? {
        val entry = cache[key] ?: return null
        if (System.currentTimeMillis() - entry.timestamp > expirationMs) {
            cache.remove(key)
            return null
        }
        return entry.value
    }

    @Synchronized
    fun put(key: K, value: V) {
        if (cache.size >= maxSize) {
            val oldest = cache.entries.first()
            cache.remove(oldest.key)
        }
        cache[key] = CacheEntry(value, System.currentTimeMillis())
    }

    @Synchronized
    fun invalidate(key: K) = cache.remove(key)

    @Synchronized
    fun invalidateAll() = cache.clear()
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 6. PAGINATION WITH PAGING 3
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Paging 3 = Jetpack library for loading data in pages.
 *
 * Components:
 *   PagingSource     → loads pages from a single data source
 *   RemoteMediator   → loads from network, caches in DB
 *   Pager            → creates a Flow<PagingData>
 *   PagingDataAdapter → RecyclerView adapter (or collectAsLazyPagingItems in Compose)
 */

class ArticlePagingSource(
    private val api: ArticleApiService,
    private val query: String
) : PagingSource<Int, Article>() {

    override suspend fun load(params: LoadParams<Int>): LoadResult<Int, Article> {
        val page = params.key ?: 1
        return try {
            val response = api.getArticles(page, params.loadSize)
            if (response.isSuccessful) {
                val body = response.body()!!
                LoadResult.Page(
                    data = body.articles.map { it.toDomain() },
                    prevKey = if (page == 1) null else page - 1,
                    nextKey = if (body.hasMore) page + 1 else null
                )
            } else {
                LoadResult.Error(HttpException(response))
            }
        } catch (e: IOException) {
            LoadResult.Error(e)
        }
    }

    override fun getRefreshKey(state: PagingState<Int, Article>): Int? {
        return state.anchorPosition?.let { anchorPosition ->
            state.closestPageToPosition(anchorPosition)?.prevKey?.plus(1)
                ?: state.closestPageToPosition(anchorPosition)?.nextKey?.minus(1)
        }
    }
}

// ViewModel with Paging
class PaginatedViewModel(private val api: ArticleApiService) : ViewModel() {
    val articles: Flow<PagingData<Article>> = Pager(
        config = PagingConfig(
            pageSize = 20,
            prefetchDistance = 5,   // Load next page when 5 items from end
            enablePlaceholders = false,
            initialLoadSize = 40    // First page loads more
        ),
        pagingSourceFactory = { ArticlePagingSource(api, "") }
    ).flow.cachedIn(viewModelScope)  // Survives config changes
}

// Compose UI with Paging
@Composable
fun PaginatedList(viewModel: PaginatedViewModel = hiltViewModel()) {
    val articles = viewModel.articles.collectAsLazyPagingItems()

    LazyColumn {
        items(articles.itemCount, key = { articles[it]?.id ?: it }) { index ->
            articles[index]?.let { article ->
                ArticleItem(article)
            }
        }

        // Loading states
        when (articles.loadState.append) {
            is LoadState.Loading -> item { CircularProgressIndicator() }
            is LoadState.Error -> item {
                RetryButton(onClick = { articles.retry() })
            }
            else -> {}
        }
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 7. NETWORK CONNECTIVITY MONITORING
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

class NetworkMonitor(context: Context) {
    private val connectivityManager =
        context.getSystemService(Context.CONNECTIVITY_SERVICE) as ConnectivityManager

    val isOnline: Flow<Boolean> = callbackFlow {
        val callback = object : ConnectivityManager.NetworkCallback() {
            override fun onAvailable(network: Network) { trySend(true) }
            override fun onLost(network: Network) { trySend(false) }
            override fun onCapabilitiesChanged(
                network: Network,
                networkCapabilities: NetworkCapabilities
            ) {
                val hasInternet = networkCapabilities.hasCapability(
                    NetworkCapabilities.NET_CAPABILITY_INTERNET
                )
                trySend(hasInternet)
            }
        }

        val request = NetworkRequest.Builder()
            .addCapability(NetworkCapabilities.NET_CAPABILITY_INTERNET)
            .build()
        connectivityManager.registerNetworkCallback(request, callback)

        // Emit current state
        val currentNetwork = connectivityManager.activeNetwork
        val capabilities = connectivityManager.getNetworkCapabilities(currentNetwork)
        trySend(capabilities?.hasCapability(NetworkCapabilities.NET_CAPABILITY_INTERNET) == true)

        awaitClose { connectivityManager.unregisterNetworkCallback(callback) }
    }.distinctUntilChanged()
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 8. WEBSOCKET & SERVER-SENT EVENTS
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * WebSocket = full-duplex, persistent connection between client and server.
 * SSE (Server-Sent Events) = one-way stream from server to client over HTTP.
 *
 * When to use what:
 *   WebSocket → bidirectional real-time (chat, gaming, collaborative editing)
 *   SSE       → server-push only (live feeds, notifications, stock tickers)
 *   Polling   → low-frequency updates, simplest to implement, highest latency
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: Google favors WebSocket for bidirectional and SSE for
 *    unidirectional streams. Know the trade-offs (connection overhead, proxy
 *    support, automatic reconnection).
 *
 * ⚠️ SSE advantages over WebSocket:
 *    - Built-in reconnection and event ID tracking
 *    - Works through HTTP proxies without special config
 *    - Simpler server implementation
 *    ⚠️ SSE limitations: text-only (no binary), unidirectional
 */

// WebSocket client with OkHttp
class ArticleWebSocketClient(
    private val okHttpClient: OkHttpClient,
    private val onMessage: (String) -> Unit,
    private val onConnectionChange: (Boolean) -> Unit
) {
    private var webSocket: WebSocket? = null
    private var retryCount = 0
    private val maxRetries = 5
    private val scope = CoroutineScope(Dispatchers.IO + SupervisorJob())

    fun connect() {
        val request = Request.Builder()
            .url("wss://api.example.com/ws/articles")
            .build()

        webSocket = okHttpClient.newWebSocket(request, object : WebSocketListener() {
            override fun onOpen(webSocket: WebSocket, response: okhttp3.Response) {
                retryCount = 0
                onConnectionChange(true)
                // Send auth or subscribe message
                webSocket.send("""{"type":"subscribe","channel":"articles"}""")
            }

            override fun onMessage(webSocket: WebSocket, text: String) {
                onMessage(text)
            }

            override fun onClosing(webSocket: WebSocket, code: Int, reason: String) {
                webSocket.close(1000, null)
                onConnectionChange(false)
            }

            override fun onFailure(
                webSocket: WebSocket,
                t: Throwable,
                response: okhttp3.Response?
            ) {
                onConnectionChange(false)
                reconnectWithBackoff()
            }
        })
    }

    private fun reconnectWithBackoff() {
        if (retryCount >= maxRetries) return
        val delayMs = (1000L * (1 shl retryCount)).coerceAtMost(30_000L) // Exponential, cap 30s
        retryCount++
        scope.launch {
            delay(delayMs)
            connect()
        }
    }

    fun send(message: String) {
        webSocket?.send(message)
    }

    fun disconnect() {
        webSocket?.close(1000, "Client disconnect")
        scope.cancel()
    }
}

// Server-Sent Events (SSE) with OkHttp
class SseClient(private val okHttpClient: OkHttpClient) {

    fun observeEvents(url: String): Flow<SseEvent> = callbackFlow {
        val request = Request.Builder()
            .url(url)
            .header("Accept", "text/event-stream")
            .build()

        val call = okHttpClient.newCall(request)
        val response = call.execute()
        val source = response.body?.source() ?: run {
            close()
            return@callbackFlow
        }

        var eventType = ""
        val dataBuilder = StringBuilder()

        try {
            while (!source.exhausted()) {
                val line = source.readUtf8Line() ?: break
                when {
                    line.startsWith("event:") -> eventType = line.removePrefix("event:").trim()
                    line.startsWith("data:") -> dataBuilder.append(line.removePrefix("data:").trim())
                    line.isEmpty() && dataBuilder.isNotEmpty() -> {
                        trySend(SseEvent(eventType, dataBuilder.toString()))
                        eventType = ""
                        dataBuilder.clear()
                    }
                }
            }
        } finally {
            response.close()
        }

        awaitClose { call.cancel() }
    }
}

data class SseEvent(val event: String, val data: String)


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 9. REMOTEMEDIATOR (OFFLINE-FIRST PAGING) — SUMMARY
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * RemoteMediator = bridge between Paging 3 and a remote data source.
 * It loads data from the network and caches it in Room.
 * PagingSource then reads from Room (single source of truth).
 *
 * Flow:
 *   LazyColumn requests page → Pager asks PagingSource (Room)
 *     → if Room has data → display
 *     → if Room exhausted → RemoteMediator.load() called
 *       → fetches from API → inserts into Room → PagingSource emits new data
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: This is the Google-recommended pattern for offline-first
 *    paginated lists. Know the full flow end-to-end — it's a very common
 *    SDE-2 system design question.
 *
 * ⚠️ Key: The DB transaction in load() must be atomic — delete old data
 *    (on REFRESH) and insert new data in a single transaction.
 *
 * → See 05_data_persistence.kt § "PAGING 3 + ROOM" for the full
 *   RemoteMediator implementation, RemoteKey entity, DAO, Pager wiring,
 *   and Compose UI with LazyPagingItems.
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 10. API DESIGN BEST PRACTICES
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * REST Versioning:
 *   - URL versioning: /api/v1/articles  (most common, Google uses this)
 *   - Header versioning: Accept: application/vnd.api+json;version=1
 *   - Query param: /api/articles?version=1
 *
 * Pagination Contracts:
 *
 *   Offset-based: ?page=3&limit=20
 *     ✅ Simple, supports random page access
 *     ❌ Inconsistent when data changes (duplicates/gaps on insert/delete)
 *
 *   Cursor-based: ?cursor=abc123&limit=20
 *     ✅ Consistent results even with data changes
 *     ✅ Better performance (no OFFSET scan on DB side)
 *     ❌ No random page access, forward-only (or with prev_cursor)
 *     💡 Google/Twitter/Slack all use cursor-based pagination
 *
 * Error Envelope Pattern:
 *   Consistent error structure across all endpoints:
 */

// Standardized error envelope
@Serializable
data class ApiErrorEnvelope(
    val error: ApiErrorDetail
)

@Serializable
data class ApiErrorDetail(
    val code: String,             // Machine-readable: "ARTICLE_NOT_FOUND"
    val message: String,          // Human-readable: "The article was not found"
    val target: String? = null,   // Which field caused the error
    val details: List<ApiErrorDetail>? = null  // Nested validation errors
)

/**
 * Idempotency Keys:
 *   Problem: network timeout on POST → did the server process it?
 *   Solution: client sends a unique key; server deduplicates.
 *
 *   Client sends: POST /orders  Header: Idempotency-Key: <UUID>
 *   Server: if key seen before → return cached response (no duplicate order)
 *           if key is new → process and store response keyed by Idempotency-Key
 *
 *   ⚠️ Critical for payment, order creation, any non-idempotent mutation.
 *
 * gRPC (relevant at Google):
 *   - Protocol Buffers (protobuf) for serialization — smaller, faster than JSON
 *   - HTTP/2 based — multiplexing, header compression, bidirectional streaming
 *   - Strongly typed service contracts (.proto files)
 *   - Google uses gRPC extensively for internal microservice communication
 *   - On Android: use grpc-kotlin for coroutine-based stubs
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: Mentioning gRPC in a Google interview shows awareness of
 *    their tech stack. Know basic trade-offs vs REST (binary vs text, streaming
 *    support, tooling maturity, browser support).
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// PRACTICE QUESTIONS
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * ❓ Q1: What's the difference between Application and Network interceptors?
 *    A: Application interceptors: called once, see original request, can short-circuit.
 *       Network interceptors: called per network request (including redirects),
 *       can access Connection, see request after OkHttp modifications.
 *
 * ❓ Q2: How do you handle token refresh in a multi-threaded environment?
 *    A: Use OkHttp Authenticator or synchronize the refresh.
 *       With an interceptor: check 401, synchronize refresh (Mutex or synchronized),
 *       check if another thread already refreshed, retry with new token.
 *
 * ❓ Q3: How would you implement offline-first with Paging 3?
 *    A: Use RemoteMediator. It fetches from API, stores in Room.
 *       PagingSource reads from Room. UI always sees local data.
 *       RemoteMediator handles network fetch + DB upsert.
 *
 * ❓ Q4: What is ETag caching?
 *    A: Server sends ETag header with response. Client sends If-None-Match
 *       on next request. If data hasn't changed, server returns 304 (Not Modified),
 *       client uses cached version. Saves bandwidth, reduces server load.
 *
 * ❓ Q5: Retrofit with coroutines — what happens on network error?
 *    A: If the function returns Response<T>, you get a Response object (check isSuccessful).
 *       If it returns T directly, network errors throw IOException/HttpException.
 *       Always wrap in try-catch or safeApiCall.
 *
 * ❓ Q6: When would you choose WebSocket vs SSE vs polling?
 *    A: WebSocket: bidirectional real-time (chat, multiplayer). Full-duplex,
 *       binary + text support, but requires explicit reconnection logic.
 *       SSE: server-push only (live feeds, notifications). Built-in reconnect,
 *       works through HTTP proxies, text-only. Simpler than WebSocket.
 *       Polling: simplest, works everywhere, highest latency. Use for
 *       low-frequency updates where real-time isn't critical.
 *
 * ❓ Q7: Walk through how RemoteMediator coordinates network and local cache.
 *    A: Pager reads from Room via PagingSource. When Room data is exhausted,
 *       Pager calls RemoteMediator.load(). RemoteMediator fetches the next
 *       page from the API, then in a single DB transaction inserts the new
 *       data and remote keys into Room. Room's PagingSource is invalidated
 *       automatically (via InvalidationTracker), emitting the new page to the UI.
 *       On REFRESH, old data and keys are cleared before inserting.
 *       initialize() can skip refresh if cache is fresh (e.g., < 1 hour old).
 */

07 Android Internals

07_android_internals.kt · Kotlin reference

/**
 * ============================================================================
 * 07 — ANDROID INTERNALS
 * Google SDE-2 Interview Prep
 * ============================================================================
 *
 * Topics Covered:
 *   1. Android Process Model
 *   2. Low Memory Killer (LMK)
 *   3. Binder IPC Framework
 *   4. Handler / Looper / MessageQueue
 *   5. App Startup Flow (Cold / Warm / Hot)
 *   6. Activity Launch Sequence (Zygote → Activity)
 *   7. APK Structure & Build Process
 *   8. ART Runtime & Compilation (AOT, JIT, Profiles)
 *   9. View Rendering Pipeline
 *  10. ANRs (Application Not Responding)
 *  11. Memory Management Depth (Heap, GC, onTrimMemory)
 *
 * ============================================================================
 */

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 1. ANDROID PROCESS MODEL
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Each Android app runs in its OWN Linux process with its OWN Dalvik/ART VM.
 *
 * Process isolation provides:
 *   - Security: one app can't access another's memory
 *   - Stability: one app crashing doesn't bring down others
 *   - Resource management: OS can kill individual processes
 *
 * Components in the same app share the same process by default.
 * You can assign components to separate processes via android:process in manifest.
 *
 * ❓ Interview Question: When would you use multiple processes?
 *    A: WebView (isolate crashes), heavy background services (separate heap),
 *       push notification handling (lightweight process for messages).
 *    ⚠️ Trade-off: Each process gets its own heap, singletons, and static state.
 *       Data sharing requires IPC (Binder, ContentProvider, files).
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 2. LOW MEMORY KILLER (LMK)
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Android kills processes based on priority to free memory.
 *
 * Process priority (high → low, killed last → first):
 *
 *   1. Foreground Process
 *      - Activity in onResume (user interacting)
 *      - Service bound to foreground Activity
 *      - BroadcastReceiver executing onReceive()
 *
 *   2. Visible Process
 *      - Activity in onPause (partially visible, e.g., behind dialog)
 *      - Service bound to a visible Activity
 *
 *   3. Service Process
 *      - Running a started Service (not bound, not foreground)
 *      - After ~30 minutes, demoted to cached
 *
 *   4. Cached (Background) Process
 *      - Activity in onStop (not visible)
 *      - Kept for fast switching, but expendable
 *      - LRU list — oldest killed first
 *
 *   5. Empty Process
 *      - No active components
 *      - Kept only for caching purposes
 *      - Killed first when memory needed
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: Explain how your architecture handles process death.
 *    SavedStateHandle, Room as SSOT, WorkManager for persistent work.
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 3. BINDER IPC FRAMEWORK
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Binder = Android's inter-process communication (IPC) mechanism.
 *
 * How it works:
 *   1. Client calls a method on a proxy object
 *   2. Proxy serializes the call into a Parcel (marshalling)
 *   3. Parcel is sent to the Binder kernel driver
 *   4. Kernel driver forwards it to the server process
 *   5. Server deserializes (unmarshalling) and executes
 *   6. Response follows the same path back
 *
 * Everything in Android uses Binder under the hood:
 *   - startActivity()   → talks to ActivityManagerService via Binder
 *   - bindService()     → returns an IBinder to communicate
 *   - ContentResolver   → talks to ContentProvider via Binder
 *   - Intents           → serialized via Binder (hence the ~1MB limit)
 *
 * AIDL (Android Interface Definition Language):
 *   - Generates Binder boilerplate code
 *   - Used for cross-process service communication
 *   - Methods can be: oneway (async), in/out/inout params
 *
 * ⚠️ Binder Transaction Limit: ~1MB per process for ALL concurrent transactions.
 *    TransactionTooLargeException if exceeded (large Bundles, Bitmaps in Intents).
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 4. HANDLER / LOOPER / MESSAGEQUEUE
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * The foundation of Android's threading model:
 *
 *   Looper       → infinite loop that processes messages from a queue
 *   MessageQueue → FIFO queue of Messages and Runnables
 *   Handler      → sends Messages/Runnables to a specific Looper's queue
 *
 * Main Thread = a thread with a Looper (created by ActivityThread.main()).
 *
 * Flow:
 *   Handler.post(runnable) → enqueues Runnable in MessageQueue
 *   Looper.loop() → dequeues and dispatches to Handler.handleMessage()
 *
 * ❓ Interview Question: How does runOnUiThread work?
 *    Answer: It checks if current thread is main thread.
 *    If yes, runs immediately. If no, posts to main Looper's Handler.
 *
 * ❓ Follow-up: How does Dispatchers.Main work under the hood?
 *    Answer: It uses a Handler attached to the main Looper to post
 *    coroutine resumption to the main thread's MessageQueue.
 */

// How Handler/Looper work conceptually
class HandlerLooperExample {

    // Creating a background thread with a Looper
    fun createBackgroundLooper() {
        val handlerThread = HandlerThread("BackgroundThread")
        handlerThread.start()

        val backgroundHandler = Handler(handlerThread.looper)
        val mainHandler = Handler(Looper.getMainLooper())

        backgroundHandler.post {
            // This runs on the background thread
            val result = heavyComputation()

            mainHandler.post {
                // This runs on the main thread
                updateUI(result)
            }
        }
    }

    // This is essentially what Dispatchers.Main does
    fun postToMainThread(block: () -> Unit) {
        val mainHandler = Handler(Looper.getMainLooper())
        mainHandler.post(block)
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 5. APP STARTUP FLOW
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Cold Start (slowest):
 *   1. User taps app icon
 *   2. Linux kernel creates a new process (forked from Zygote)
 *   3. ART creates the Application object
 *   4. Application.onCreate() runs
 *   5. ActivityThread creates the main Activity
 *   6. Activity.onCreate() → onStart() → onResume()
 *   7. First frame drawn → app is interactive
 *
 * Warm Start:
 *   - Process exists but Activity was destroyed
 *   - Skips steps 2-4, starts from step 5
 *   - Faster because Application is already initialized
 *
 * Hot Start:
 *   - Process AND Activity exist (in onStop)
 *   - Just brings Activity to foreground
 *   - onRestart() → onStart() → onResume()
 *
 * 💡 Google Metric: Cold start should be < 500ms for good UX.
 *    Time To Initial Display (TTID) and Time To Full Display (TTFD).
 *
 * Optimizing Cold Start:
 *   - Lazy-initialize libraries in Application.onCreate()
 *   - Use App Startup library (Jetpack) for ContentProvider initialization
 *   - Defer non-critical initialization to background
 *   - Use Baseline Profiles for AOT compilation of hot paths
 *   - Minimize work in Activity.onCreate()
 */

// Optimized Application class
class OptimizedApplication : Application() {
    override fun onCreate() {
        super.onCreate()

        // Only critical initialization here
        initializeCrashReporting()  // Must be first

        // Defer everything else
        val backgroundScope = CoroutineScope(Dispatchers.Default + SupervisorJob())
        backgroundScope.launch {
            initializeAnalytics()
            initializeRemoteConfig()
            preloadFonts()
        }
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 6. ZYGOTE & ACTIVITY LAUNCH SEQUENCE
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Zygote = template process that every Android app is forked from.
 *
 * Why Zygote?
 *   - Pre-loads common framework classes and resources
 *   - fork() is fast (copy-on-write memory)
 *   - New app process gets pre-warmed ART runtime
 *
 * Full Activity Launch Sequence:
 *
 *   Launcher → startActivity(intent)
 *       → ActivityManagerService (system process, Binder IPC)
 *           → resolves Intent, checks permissions
 *           → if process doesn't exist: tells Zygote to fork
 *       → Zygote forks a new process
 *           → New process: ActivityThread.main() starts
 *           → Creates Looper, Handler for main thread
 *           → Binds to ActivityManagerService
 *       → AMS tells new process to create Application
 *           → Application.onCreate()
 *       → AMS tells process to launch Activity
 *           → Activity.onCreate() → onStart() → onResume()
 *       → WindowManager adds Activity's window
 *       → First frame rendered (TTID)
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 7. APK STRUCTURE & BUILD PROCESS
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * APK Contents:
 *   AndroidManifest.xml    → compiled binary XML
 *   classes.dex            → compiled bytecode (Dalvik Executable)
 *   resources.arsc         → compiled resources table
 *   res/                   → resources not compiled into resources.arsc
 *   lib/                   → native libraries (arm64-v8a, armeabi-v7a, x86_64)
 *   assets/                → raw files (accessed via AssetManager)
 *   META-INF/              → APK signature
 *
 * Build Process:
 *   .kt/.java → kotlinc/javac → .class → D8 → .dex
 *   R8 (release) → optimization, shrinking, obfuscation
 *   Resources → AAPT2 → compiled resources + R.java
 *   All combined → APK → zipalign → APK signing
 *
 * App Bundle (AAB):
 *   - Google Play generates optimized APKs per device
 *   - Split APKs: base + config (density, ABI, language)
 *   - Smaller downloads: user only gets what they need
 *   - Dynamic Feature Modules: download features on demand
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 8. ART RUNTIME
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * ART (Android Runtime) replaced Dalvik in Android 5.0.
 *
 * Compilation strategies:
 *
 *   AOT (Ahead-Of-Time):
 *     - Compiles dex to native code at install time
 *     - Faster startup, higher storage cost
 *     - Used for frequently-used code paths
 *
 *   JIT (Just-In-Time):
 *     - Compiles hot methods at runtime
 *     - Adapts to actual usage patterns
 *     - Profiles stored for future AOT compilation
 *
 *   Profile-Guided Compilation:
 *     - JIT collects profiles of hot methods
 *     - On device idle/charging, AOT-compiles those methods
 *     - Baseline Profiles (developer-provided) + Cloud Profiles (from Play)
 *
 * Garbage Collection:
 *   - Concurrent, generational GC
 *   - Young generation: small, collected frequently (minor GC)
 *   - Old generation: large, collected rarely (major GC)
 *   - ⚠️ GC pauses can cause jank (skipped frames)
 *   - Avoid excessive allocations in onDraw() / composable recomposition
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 9. VIEW RENDERING PIPELINE
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * How a frame is rendered (View system):
 *
 *   VSYNC signal (every 16.6ms for 60fps)
 *       → Choreographer dispatches frame callback
 *           → Input events processed
 *           → Animations updated
 *           → Measure pass (top-down, each view measures children)
 *           → Layout pass (top-down, each view positions children)
 *           → Draw pass (top-down, each view draws itself)
 *       → RenderThread → GPU renders display list
 *       → SurfaceFlinger composites all surfaces
 *       → Display shows frame
 *
 * Frame budget: 16.6ms (60fps) or 8.3ms (120fps)
 * If frame takes longer → dropped frame → jank
 *
 * Common causes of jank:
 *   - Deep view hierarchies (too many measure/layout passes)
 *   - Overdraw (drawing the same pixel multiple times)
 *   - Main thread blocking (IO, heavy computation)
 *   - GC pauses from excessive allocation
 *   - Large bitmap decoding on main thread
 *
 * Compose rendering is different:
 *   Composition → Layout → Drawing (3 phases)
 *   Uses a slot table instead of a view tree
 *   Can skip phases if state hasn't changed
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 10. ANRs (APPLICATION NOT RESPONDING)
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * ANR = system dialog shown when the app is unresponsive to user input.
 *
 * ANR Triggers:
 *   - Input dispatch timeout: 5 seconds (touch/key event not handled)
 *   - BroadcastReceiver timeout: 10 seconds (onReceive not finished)
 *   - Service timeout: 20 seconds (onCreate/onStartCommand not finished)
 *   - ContentProvider timeout: published within 10 seconds of creation
 *
 * Root Cause: almost always main thread blocking.
 *
 * Common Causes:
 *   1. Synchronous disk I/O on main thread (SharedPreferences.commit(), DB queries)
 *   2. Synchronous network call on main thread
 *   3. Long-running computation on main thread (JSON parsing, bitmap decode)
 *   4. Lock contention — main thread waiting on a lock held by another thread
 *   5. Deadlock between main thread and a worker thread
 *   6. Slow BroadcastReceiver (doing IO in onReceive)
 *   7. Binder call to a slow or hung remote service
 *
 * Diagnosis Tools:
 *   - /data/anr/traces.txt → full thread dump at time of ANR
 *   - StrictMode → detects disk/network IO on main thread during development
 *   - Systrace / Perfetto → system-level trace showing thread activity
 *   - Android Studio Profiler → CPU trace reveals blocking calls
 *   - Google Play Console → ANR clusters with stack traces
 *
 * ⚠️ Google Play ANR Thresholds:
 *   - User-perceived ANR rate > 0.47% → "bad behavior" warning
 *   - User-perceived ANR rate > 8% → app may be deprioritized in Play Store
 *   - These are measured per active daily users, not sessions
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: When asked "how do you prevent ANRs?", don't just say
 *    "move work off main thread." Discuss StrictMode for detection, coroutines
 *    with Dispatchers.IO for I/O, and monitoring with Play Console vitals.
 */

// StrictMode setup for detecting main thread violations in debug builds
class DebugApplication : Application() {
    override fun onCreate() {
        super.onCreate()

        if (BuildConfig.DEBUG) {
            StrictMode.setThreadPolicy(
                StrictMode.ThreadPolicy.Builder()
                    .detectDiskReads()
                    .detectDiskWrites()
                    .detectNetwork()
                    .detectCustomSlowCalls()
                    .penaltyLog()        // Log to Logcat
                    .penaltyFlashScreen() // Red border flash on violation
                    .build()
            )

            StrictMode.setVmPolicy(
                StrictMode.VmPolicy.Builder()
                    .detectLeakedClosableObjects()
                    .detectLeakedSqlLiteObjects()
                    .detectActivityLeaks()
                    .penaltyLog()
                    .build()
            )
        }
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 11. MEMORY MANAGEMENT DEPTH
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * ART Heap Structure:
 *
 *   Young Generation (Nursery):
 *     - Where newly allocated objects live
 *     - Small, collected frequently (minor/young GC)
 *     - Most objects die young (generational hypothesis)
 *     - Uses a semi-space or copying collector
 *
 *   Old Generation (Tenured):
 *     - Objects that survived multiple young GCs are promoted here
 *     - Larger, collected less frequently (major GC)
 *     - Uses a concurrent mark-sweep or compacting collector
 *
 *   Large Object Space (LOS):
 *     - Objects > 12KB (typically large arrays, bitmaps)
 *     - Allocated separately to avoid fragmentation
 *     - Collected as part of major GC
 *
 * Native Heap vs Dalvik/ART Heap:
 *   - ART heap: managed by GC, holds Java/Kotlin objects
 *   - Native heap: unmanaged (malloc/free), holds native allocations
 *   - Bitmaps (API 26+): allocated on native heap, not ART heap
 *   - JNI allocations, NDK code, graphics buffers → native heap
 *   - Native leaks don't show in Memory Profiler heap dump — use malloc debug
 *
 * android:largeHeap="true":
 *   - Requests a larger Dalvik/ART heap (typically 256-512MB vs 128-256MB)
 *   ⚠️ Why it's usually wrong:
 *     - Does NOT fix memory leaks — just delays OOM
 *     - Longer GC pauses (more heap to scan)
 *     - System may kill your app sooner (uses more memory overall)
 *     - Legitimate use cases: photo/video editors, games with large assets
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: If asked about memory optimization, discuss the heap
 *    structure, then onTrimMemory for responding to pressure, and finally
 *    profiling tools (Memory Profiler, LeakCanary). Mentioning largeHeap
 *    as an anti-pattern shows depth.
 */

/**
 * onTrimMemory / ComponentCallbacks2:
 *
 * Called by the system when memory is low. Your app should respond
 * by releasing non-critical resources to avoid being killed.
 *
 * Levels (increasing severity):
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_RUNNING_MODERATE (5)
 *     → App is running, device is getting low on memory
 *     → Consider releasing non-essential caches
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_RUNNING_LOW (10)
 *     → App is running, device is quite low on memory
 *     → Release caches and reduce memory footprint
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_RUNNING_CRITICAL (15)
 *     → App is running, device is critically low
 *     → Release everything non-essential immediately
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_UI_HIDDEN (20)
 *     → App's UI is no longer visible
 *     → Release UI-related resources (image caches, view caches)
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_BACKGROUND (40)
 *     → App is in the LRU background list
 *     → Release as much as possible to survive longer
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_MODERATE (60)
 *     → App is in the middle of the LRU list
 *     → Getting close to being killed
 *
 *   TRIM_MEMORY_COMPLETE (80)
 *     → App is near the end of the LRU list
 *     → Will be killed next if memory isn't freed
 */

class MemoryAwareApplication : Application() {  // Application already implements ComponentCallbacks2

    private val imageCache = LruCache<String, Bitmap>(calculateMemoryCacheSize())

    override fun onTrimMemory(level: Int) {
        super.onTrimMemory(level)

        when {
            level >= ComponentCallbacks2.TRIM_MEMORY_COMPLETE -> {
                // About to be killed — release everything
                imageCache.evictAll()
                clearAllCaches()
            }
            level >= ComponentCallbacks2.TRIM_MEMORY_BACKGROUND -> {
                // In background LRU — release most caches
                imageCache.trimToSize(imageCache.maxSize() / 4)
                clearNonEssentialCaches()
            }
            level >= ComponentCallbacks2.TRIM_MEMORY_UI_HIDDEN -> {
                // UI hidden — release UI caches
                imageCache.trimToSize(imageCache.maxSize() / 2)
            }
            level >= ComponentCallbacks2.TRIM_MEMORY_RUNNING_CRITICAL -> {
                // Running but critical — reduce aggressively
                imageCache.trimToSize(imageCache.maxSize() / 8)
            }
            level >= ComponentCallbacks2.TRIM_MEMORY_RUNNING_LOW -> {
                // Running but low — reduce caches
                imageCache.trimToSize(imageCache.maxSize() / 4)
            }
        }
    }

    private fun calculateMemoryCacheSize(): Int {
        val maxMemory = Runtime.getRuntime().maxMemory() / 1024  // KB
        return (maxMemory / 8).toInt()  // Use 1/8th of available memory
    }

    private fun clearAllCaches() { /* release all in-memory caches */ }
    private fun clearNonEssentialCaches() { /* release non-UI caches */ }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// PRACTICE QUESTIONS
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * ❓ Q1: What is Zygote and why does Android use it?
 *    A: Zygote is a special process started at boot that pre-loads framework
 *       classes and resources. All app processes are forked from Zygote via
 *       fork(), which is fast because of copy-on-write memory semantics.
 *       This means each new app starts with framework already in memory.
 *
 * ❓ Q2: Explain the Binder transaction size limit and its implications.
 *    A: ~1MB per process for ALL concurrent transactions. This affects:
 *       - Intent extras (don't put large data in Intents)
 *       - onSaveInstanceState Bundles (keep small)
 *       - ContentProvider queries (use ContentResolver with cursors, not full data)
 *       Exceeding causes TransactionTooLargeException.
 *
 * ❓ Q3: Why can't you touch the UI from a background thread?
 *    A: Android's View system is NOT thread-safe. ViewRootImpl.checkThread()
 *       verifies all UI operations happen on the thread that created the view
 *       hierarchy (main thread). This is enforced to prevent race conditions
 *       during measure/layout/draw passes.
 *
 * ❓ Q4: How do Baseline Profiles improve app performance?
 *    A: They provide a list of "hot" classes and methods to the ART compiler.
 *       These are AOT-compiled at install time, so they're native code on
 *       first launch. Without profiles, JIT compilation causes slower first
 *       run. Can improve startup by 20-30%.
 *
 * ❓ Q5: What happens when you call startActivity()?
 *    A: Intent is sent via Binder to ActivityManagerService (AMS) in the
 *       system_server process. AMS resolves the Intent, checks permissions,
 *       determines target component, and instructs the target process to
 *       create/resume the Activity. If the process doesn't exist, AMS
 *       tells Zygote to fork a new one first.
 *
 * ❓ Q6: Your app has a high ANR rate. How do you diagnose and fix it?
 *    A: 1. Check Play Console ANR clusters for stack traces.
 *       2. Look at /data/anr/traces.txt for the thread dump — find what the
 *          main thread was blocked on (lock, I/O, binder call).
 *       3. Enable StrictMode in debug to catch disk/network on main thread.
 *       4. Use Systrace/Perfetto to see main thread activity around the ANR.
 *       5. Common fixes: move I/O to Dispatchers.IO, use
 *          SharedPreferences.apply() instead of commit(), use async BroadcastReceiver
 *          with goAsync() for long work, avoid holding locks across threads.
 *
 * ❓ Q7: Explain onTrimMemory levels and how you'd respond to memory pressure.
 *    A: onTrimMemory is called with increasing severity levels (5→80).
 *       RUNNING_* levels (5/10/15): app is foreground but device is low —
 *       progressively reduce caches. UI_HIDDEN (20): release UI resources.
 *       BACKGROUND/MODERATE/COMPLETE (40/60/80): app is in background LRU,
 *       risk of being killed — release everything non-essential.
 *       Implementation: use LruCache.trimToSize() to progressively shrink,
 *       clear image/data caches, release held resources. Responding properly
 *       keeps your app alive longer in the background.
 */

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 1. UI PERFORMANCE — JANK & OVERDRAW
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Jank = dropped frames. Occurs when frame takes longer than 16.6ms (60fps).
 *
 * Common Causes:
 *   1. Main thread blocking (IO, heavy computation)
 *   2. Deep view hierarchies (nested LinearLayouts)
 *   3. Overdraw (multiple layers drawing on the same pixel)
 *   4. Expensive onDraw() operations
 *   5. Layout thrashing (requestLayout() called too often)
 *   6. Large bitmap decoding on main thread
 *
 * Overdraw Levels:
 *   No overdraw  (1x) → white/no tint
 *   1x overdraw  (2x) → blue
 *   2x overdraw  (3x) → green
 *   3x overdraw  (4x) → red — needs optimization
 *
 * Fixing Overdraw:
 *   - Remove unnecessary backgrounds (window background under Activity background)
 *   - Use clipRect() in custom views
 *   - Flatten view hierarchies
 *   - Use android:windowBackground for splash instead of Activity background
 */

// ❌ Deep hierarchy — slow layout passes
/*
<LinearLayout>
    <LinearLayout>
        <LinearLayout>
            <ImageView />
            <LinearLayout>
                <TextView />
                <TextView />
            </LinearLayout>
        </LinearLayout>
    </LinearLayout>
</LinearLayout>
*/

// ✅ Flat hierarchy with ConstraintLayout
/*
<ConstraintLayout>
    <ImageView />
    <TextView />    <!-- constrained to ImageView -->
    <TextView />    <!-- constrained to first TextView -->
</ConstraintLayout>
*/


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 2. RECYCLERVIEW OPTIMIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * RecyclerView best practices for smooth scrolling:
 */

// ✅ Use DiffUtil for efficient updates (no notifyDataSetChanged)
class ArticleDiffCallback : DiffUtil.ItemCallback<Article>() {
    override fun areItemsTheSame(old: Article, new: Article): Boolean {
        return old.id == new.id
    }

    override fun areContentsTheSame(old: Article, new: Article): Boolean {
        return old == new  // data class equals
    }

    // Partial updates — only change specific fields
    override fun getChangePayload(old: Article, new: Article): Any? {
        return buildList {
            if (old.isBookmarked != new.isBookmarked) add("bookmark")
            if (old.likeCount != new.likeCount) add("likes")
        }.takeIf { it.isNotEmpty() }
    }
}

class ArticleAdapter : ListAdapter<Article, ArticleViewHolder>(ArticleDiffCallback()) {

    override fun onCreateViewHolder(parent: ViewGroup, viewType: Int): ArticleViewHolder {
        // ✅ Use ViewBinding — faster than findViewById
        val binding = ItemArticleBinding.inflate(
            LayoutInflater.from(parent.context), parent, false
        )
        return ArticleViewHolder(binding)
    }

    override fun onBindViewHolder(holder: ArticleViewHolder, position: Int) {
        holder.bind(getItem(position))
    }

    // Handle partial updates from DiffUtil payload
    override fun onBindViewHolder(
        holder: ArticleViewHolder,
        position: Int,
        payloads: MutableList<Any>
    ) {
        if (payloads.isEmpty()) {
            super.onBindViewHolder(holder, position, payloads)
            return
        }
        // Only update changed fields — much faster than full rebind
        val changes = payloads.flatMap { it as List<*> }
        val article = getItem(position)
        if ("bookmark" in changes) holder.updateBookmark(article.isBookmarked)
        if ("likes" in changes) holder.updateLikes(article.likeCount)
    }
}

/**
 * More RecyclerView optimization tips:
 *
 *   setHasFixedSize(true)     → if item size doesn't change, skip measure pass
 *   setItemViewCacheSize(n)   → keep n views in cache for fast scroll back
 *   recycledViewPool.setMaxRecycledViews() → tune pool per view type
 *   setHasStableIds(true)     → helps RecyclerView track items across updates
 *   Prefetch: RecyclerView prefetches next items during idle time (default on)
 *
 * ⚠️ Common mistakes:
 *   - Creating new objects in onBindViewHolder (allocations → GC → jank)
 *   - Setting click listeners in onBindViewHolder (use onCreateViewHolder)
 *   - Not using DiffUtil (notifyDataSetChanged redraws everything)
 *   - Nested RecyclerViews without shared RecycledViewPool
 */

// Nested RecyclerView optimization
fun setupNestedRecyclerViews(parentRecyclerView: RecyclerView) {
    val viewPool = RecyclerView.RecycledViewPool()
    // Share pool across all inner RecyclerViews
    // Each inner RecyclerView uses:
    // innerRecyclerView.setRecycledViewPool(viewPool)
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 3. COMPOSE PERFORMANCE
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Compose-specific performance optimizations:
 */

// ✅ Use key() for stable identity in LazyColumn
@Composable
fun OptimizedList(items: ImmutableList<Article>) {
    LazyColumn {
        items(
            items = items,
            key = { it.id }  // Stable identity — enables item reuse and animations
        ) { article ->
            ArticleItem(article)
        }
    }
}

// ✅ Defer state reads to avoid unnecessary recomposition
@Composable
fun ScrollingHeader() {
    val listState = rememberLazyListState()

    // ❌ Bad: reads state in composition phase
    val showButton = listState.firstVisibleItemIndex > 0

    // ✅ Good: reads state in drawing phase via derivedStateOf
    val showButton2 by remember {
        derivedStateOf { listState.firstVisibleItemIndex > 0 }
    }

    // ✅ Even better for animations: defer to layout/draw phase
    Box(modifier = Modifier.offset {
        IntOffset(0, -listState.firstVisibleItemScrollOffset)
    })
}

// ✅ Use ImmutableList for skippable recomposition
@Composable
fun StableList(items: ImmutableList<String>) {
    // Compose can skip this if items hasn't changed
    items.forEach { Text(it) }
}

// ✅ Avoid unnecessary recomposition with remember for lambdas
@Composable
fun OptimizedCallbacks(viewModel: ArticleViewModel) {
    val items by viewModel.items.collectAsStateWithLifecycle()

    LazyColumn {
        items(items, key = { it.id }) { article ->
            // ❌ Creates new lambda on every recomposition
            // ArticleItem(article, onClick = { viewModel.onClick(article.id) })

            // ✅ Stable reference — Compose can skip recomposition of ArticleItem
            val onClick = remember(article.id) { { viewModel.onClick(article.id) } }
            ArticleItem(article, onClick = onClick)
        }
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 4. MEMORY OPTIMIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Common Memory Leaks in Android:
 *
 *   1. Static reference to Activity/Context
 *      Fix: Use Application Context or WeakReference
 *
 *   2. Inner class holding implicit reference to outer Activity
 *      Fix: Use static inner class + WeakReference
 *
 *   3. Handler with Activity reference
 *      Fix: Use lifecycleScope.launch instead
 *
 *   4. Unregistered listeners (LocationManager, SensorManager, BroadcastReceiver)
 *      Fix: Unregister in onPause/onStop/onDestroyView
 *
 *   5. Fragment View binding not cleared in onDestroyView
 *      Fix: Set _binding = null in onDestroyView
 *
 *   6. Coroutine scope outliving the component
 *      Fix: Use viewModelScope, lifecycleScope, viewLifecycleOwner.lifecycleScope
 *
 *   7. ViewModel holding View/Activity reference
 *      Fix: Never pass View or Activity to ViewModel
 */

// ❌ Classic memory leak — anonymous inner class holds Activity reference
class LeakyActivity : AppCompatActivity() {
    override fun onCreate(savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
        super.onCreate(savedInstanceState)
        // ❌ Runnable holds implicit reference to this Activity
        val handler = Handler(Looper.getMainLooper())
        handler.postDelayed({
            // This keeps Activity alive for 10 seconds even after onDestroy
            updateUI()
        }, 10_000)
    }
}

// ✅ Fixed with lifecycleScope
class SafeActivity : AppCompatActivity() {
    override fun onCreate(savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
        super.onCreate(savedInstanceState)
        lifecycleScope.launch {
            delay(10_000)
            // Automatically cancelled when Activity is destroyed
            updateUI()
        }
    }
}

/**
 * Bitmap Memory Management:
 *   - Bitmaps are the #1 source of OOM crashes
 *   - A 4000x3000 photo at ARGB_8888 = 48MB of heap!
 *
 * Solutions:
 *   1. Use image loading libraries (Coil, Glide) — they handle caching + sampling
 *   2. inSampleSize — decode at reduced resolution
 *   3. Bitmap.Config.RGB_565 — half memory (no alpha), good for photos
 *   4. BitmapFactory.Options.inBitmap — reuse bitmap memory
 */

fun decodeSampledBitmap(
    resources: Resources,
    resId: Int,
    reqWidth: Int,
    reqHeight: Int
): Bitmap {
    // First pass: decode bounds only (no memory allocation)
    val options = BitmapFactory.Options().apply {
        inJustDecodeBounds = true
    }
    BitmapFactory.decodeResource(resources, resId, options)

    // Calculate sample size
    options.inSampleSize = calculateInSampleSize(options, reqWidth, reqHeight)
    options.inJustDecodeBounds = false

    return BitmapFactory.decodeResource(resources, resId, options)
}

fun calculateInSampleSize(
    options: BitmapFactory.Options,
    reqWidth: Int,
    reqHeight: Int
): Int {
    val (height, width) = options.outHeight to options.outWidth
    var sampleSize = 1
    if (height > reqHeight || width > reqWidth) {
        val halfHeight = height / 2
        val halfWidth = width / 2
        while (halfHeight / sampleSize >= reqHeight && halfWidth / sampleSize >= reqWidth) {
            sampleSize *= 2
        }
    }
    return sampleSize
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 5. BATTERY OPTIMIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Doze Mode (API 23+):
 *   When device is stationary, unplugged, screen off:
 *   - Network access suspended
 *   - Alarms deferred
 *   - GPS/Wi-Fi scanning stopped
 *   - JobScheduler/WorkManager deferred
 *   - Periodic maintenance windows allow batched work
 *
 * App Standby Buckets (API 28+):
 *   Active     → currently in use, no restrictions
 *   Working    → recently used, mild restrictions
 *   Frequent   → used regularly, more restrictions
 *   Rare       → hardly used, heavy restrictions
 *   Restricted → not used + misbehaving, very heavy restrictions
 *
 * Battery optimization checklist:
 *   ✅ Use WorkManager with constraints (not AlarmManager)
 *   ✅ Batch network requests
 *   ✅ Use Firebase Cloud Messaging (FCM) instead of polling
 *   ✅ Minimize wake locks
 *   ✅ Use JobScheduler for deferrable work
 *   ✅ Reduce GPS accuracy when possible
 *   ✅ Use Fused Location Provider
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 6. APK SIZE OPTIMIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * APK size directly impacts install conversion rates.
 * Every 6MB increase → 1% drop in install rate (Google data).
 *
 * Optimization techniques:
 *
 *   R8 (ProGuard successor):
 *     - Code shrinking — removes unused classes/methods
 *     - Obfuscation — shortens names (a, b, c)
 *     - Optimization — inlines methods, simplifies code
 *     - Resource shrinking — removes unused resources
 *
 *   App Bundle (AAB):
 *     - Dynamic delivery — users download only what they need
 *     - Split APKs — per density, ABI, language
 *     - Dynamic feature modules — on-demand module download
 *     - Typically 15-20% smaller than universal APK
 *
 *   Other techniques:
 *     - Use WebP instead of PNG (25-34% smaller)
 *     - Vector drawables instead of multiple density PNGs
 *     - Remove unused libraries
 *     - Use android.enableR8.fullMode=true in gradle.properties
 *     - Compress native libraries: android.bundle.enableUncompressedNativeLibs=true
 */

// build.gradle.kts — R8 configuration
/*
android {
    buildTypes {
        release {
            isMinifyEnabled = true       // Code shrinking
            isShrinkResources = true     // Resource shrinking
            proguardFiles(
                getDefaultProguardFile("proguard-android-optimize.txt"),
                "proguard-rules.pro"
            )
        }
    }
}
*/


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 7. STARTUP OPTIMIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Goal: Cold start < 500ms (Google benchmark)
 *
 * Techniques:
 *
 *   1. Lazy initialization:
 *      Don't initialize everything in Application.onCreate().
 *      Use by lazy {} or Hilt's @Inject for on-demand creation.
 *
 *   2. App Startup library:
 *      Replaces multiple ContentProviders (which run before Application.onCreate)
 *      with a single one that initializes libraries efficiently.
 *
 *   3. Baseline Profiles:
 *      Pre-compile critical code paths (splash → home screen).
 *      20-30% startup improvement typical.
 *
 *   4. Splash Screen API (API 31+):
 *      System-managed splash screen — shows while app initializes.
 *      Replaces custom splash Activity pattern.
 *
 *   5. Reduce main thread work:
 *      Move initialization to background threads.
 *      Use Dispatchers.Default for CPU-bound init.
 */

// App Startup library
class AnalyticsInitializer : Initializer<Analytics> {
    override fun create(context: Context): Analytics {
        return Analytics.init(context)
    }

    override fun dependencies(): List<Class<out Initializer<*>>> {
        // Declare dependencies — these initialize first
        return listOf(CrashReportingInitializer::class.java)
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 8. PROFILING TOOLS
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Android Studio Profiler:
 *
 *   CPU Profiler:
 *     - System Trace → see thread activity, frame rendering, system events
 *     - Method Trace → see exact method calls and durations
 *     - Use for: finding jank, slow methods, main thread blocking
 *
 *   Memory Profiler:
 *     - Real-time heap usage graph
 *     - Heap dump → see all live objects
 *     - Allocation tracking → see where objects are created
 *     - Use for: finding memory leaks, excessive allocations
 *
 *   Network Profiler:
 *     - Timeline of network requests
 *     - Request/response inspection
 *     - Use for: finding redundant calls, large payloads
 *
 *   Energy Profiler:
 *     - CPU, Network, GPS, Wake Lock usage
 *     - Use for: finding battery drain causes
 *
 * LeakCanary:
 *   - Automatic memory leak detection in debug builds
 *   - Detects: Activity, Fragment, ViewModel, View, Service leaks
 *   - Shows leak trace (reference chain) for debugging
 *
 * Macrobenchmark & Microbenchmark:
 *   - Macrobenchmark: measures app-level metrics (startup, scrolling)
 *   - Microbenchmark: measures code-level metrics (function timing)
 *   - Both provide deterministic, reproducible results
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 9. NETWORK OPTIMIZATION
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Connection Pooling & HTTP/2 Multiplexing:
 *   - OkHttp maintains a ConnectionPool (default: 5 idle connections, 5 min keepalive)
 *   - HTTP/2 multiplexes multiple requests over a single TCP connection
 *   - Reduces latency from TCP handshake + TLS negotiation
 *   - Configure: ConnectionPool(maxIdleConnections, keepAliveDuration, timeUnit)
 *
 * Request Batching & Deduplication:
 *   - Batch multiple small requests into one (e.g., GraphQL, batch REST endpoints)
 *   - Deduplicate identical in-flight requests (return same Deferred/Flow to callers)
 *   - OkHttp automatically deduplicates concurrent requests to the same URL with
 *     the same Cache-Control behavior
 *
 * Compression:
 *   - OkHttp adds Accept-Encoding: gzip automatically
 *   - Server must support Content-Encoding: gzip
 *   - Reduces payload size by 60-80% for JSON/text responses
 *   - Don't manually gzip if OkHttp handles transparent decompression
 *
 * Prefetching Strategies:
 *   - Prefetch next page of paginated data when user is near the end
 *   - Prefetch likely navigation targets (e.g., article detail when scrolling list)
 *   - Use WorkManager or idle time for background prefetch
 *   ⚠️ Balance: prefetching wastes bandwidth if user doesn't use the data
 *
 * Reducing Payload Size:
 *   - Request only needed fields (sparse fieldsets, GraphQL partial queries)
 *   - Use Protocol Buffers (protobuf) instead of JSON for smaller payloads
 *   - Paginate — don't fetch entire collections
 *   - Use delta sync / If-Modified-Since / ETag for incremental updates
 *
 * Image Optimization:
 *   - WebP: 25-34% smaller than PNG, supports transparency and animation
 *   - Progressive loading: show low-quality placeholder, load full-res async
 *   - Thumbnails: request server-side resized images matching display size
 *   - Coil/Glide handle disk caching, memory caching, and downsampling
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: Discuss the full stack — client-side (pooling, caching,
 *    compression) AND API-side (pagination, field selection, image CDN).
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 10. PERFETTO & SYSTRACE
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Perfetto = modern system-level tracing tool (replaces Systrace).
 * Captures kernel-level + userspace events across ALL processes.
 *
 * What Perfetto captures:
 *   - CPU scheduling (which threads run on which cores)
 *   - Binder transactions
 *   - Rendering pipeline (Choreographer, RenderThread, SurfaceFlinger)
 *   - Disk I/O, memory allocations
 *   - Custom trace sections (android.os.Trace / androidx.tracing)
 *
 * How to capture:
 *   1. Android Studio → Profiler → CPU → System Trace (uses Perfetto)
 *   2. Command line: adb shell perfetto -o /data/misc/perfetto-traces/trace.pb
 *   3. On-device: Developer Options → System Tracing
 *   4. Open traces at ui.perfetto.dev
 *
 * Reading a Perfetto trace:
 *   - Each row = a thread or process
 *   - Colored blocks = trace sections (slices)
 *   - Look for long slices on the main thread (> 16ms = jank source)
 *   - "Choreographer#doFrame" → the frame boundary
 *   - Gaps between slices = thread was sleeping or waiting
 *
 * Identifying jank:
 *   - Find frames where doFrame > 16.6ms
 *   - Check what's on the main thread during that frame:
 *     → Long measure/layout? Deep view hierarchy
 *     → Binder transaction? Slow IPC call
 *     → Lock contention? Deadlock or resource competition
 *     → Disk I/O? Blocking read on main thread
 *
 * Perfetto vs Android Studio Profiler:
 *   - Profiler: easier UI, integrated in IDE, app-scoped
 *   - Perfetto: system-wide, more detail, better for cross-process issues
 *   - Use Profiler for quick checks, Perfetto for deep investigation
 *
 * Adding custom trace sections:
 */

// Custom trace sections for identifying slow code paths
class TracedRepository(private val dao: ArticleDao, private val api: ArticleApiService) {

    suspend fun loadArticles(): List<Article> {
        // Appears as "ArticleRepository.loadArticles" in Perfetto/Systrace
        return Trace.beginAsyncSection("ArticleRepository.loadArticles", 0).let {
            try {
                val cached = traceSection("loadFromDb") { dao.getAll() }
                if (cached.isNotEmpty()) return cached

                val remote = traceSection("fetchFromApi") { api.getArticles(1).body()!! }
                val entities = remote.articles.map { it.toEntity() }
                traceSection("saveToDb") { dao.insertAll(entities) }
                entities.map { it.toDomain() }
            } finally {
                Trace.endAsyncSection("ArticleRepository.loadArticles", 0)
            }
        }
    }
}

// Helper for synchronous trace sections
inline fun <T> traceSection(label: String, block: () -> T): T {
    Trace.beginSection(label)
    return try {
        block()
    } finally {
        Trace.endSection()
    }
}


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 11. ANDROID VITALS
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Android Vitals = performance metrics dashboard in Google Play Console.
 * Google uses these metrics to determine app quality and search ranking.
 *
 * Core Vitals (affect store visibility):
 *
 *   ANR Rate:
 *     - User-perceived ANR rate (ANRs / daily active users)
 *     - Bad threshold: > 0.47%
 *     - Excessive threshold: > 8%
 *     - Main cause: main thread blocking (IO, locks, slow binder calls)
 *
 *   Crash Rate:
 *     - User-perceived crash rate
 *     - Bad threshold: > 1.09%
 *     - Excessive threshold: > 8%
 *     - Includes Java/Kotlin exceptions AND native crashes
 *
 *   Slow Rendering (Frozen Frames):
 *     - Frames that take > 700ms to render
 *     - Bad threshold: > 0.1% of sessions
 *     - Caused by main thread blocking during frame rendering
 *
 *   Excessive Wakeups:
 *     - AlarmManager wakeups that wake the device from Doze
 *     - Bad threshold: > 10 wakeups per hour
 *     - Fix: use WorkManager, avoid exact alarms unless necessary
 *
 *   Stuck Background Wake Locks:
 *     - Wake locks held for > 1 hour while in background
 *     - Fix: always set timeout on wake locks, release in finally blocks
 *
 * Using Vitals to Drive Performance Work:
 *   1. Check Play Console weekly for vitals regressions
 *   2. Correlate regressions with app version releases
 *   3. Use ANR/crash clusters → group by stack trace → prioritize by volume
 *   4. Set up alerting for threshold violations
 *   5. Track vitals improvement as a team OKR
 *
 * ⚠️ Apps with bad vitals get:
 *   - Warning badge in Play Store listing
 *   - Lower search ranking and recommendations
 *   - Potential removal from featured placements
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: Mentioning Android Vitals shows you think about production
 *    quality, not just feature development. Know the thresholds and how you'd
 *    set up a process to monitor and improve them.
 */


// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// 12. BASELINE PROFILES
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

/**
 * Baseline Profiles = developer-provided list of critical code paths for AOT compilation.
 *
 * How it works:
 *   1. Developer writes a Macrobenchmark test that exercises critical user journeys
 *   2. BaselineProfileRule records which classes/methods are touched
 *   3. Generated profile is bundled in the APK/AAB
 *   4. At install time, ART AOT-compiles these methods (instead of interpreting/JIT)
 *   5. On first launch: profiled code runs as native machine code
 *
 * Benefits:
 *   - 20-30% faster cold startup (typical improvement)
 *   - Smoother first-time scrolling (avoids JIT compilation stutters)
 *   - Consistent performance from first launch
 *   - Cloud Profiles (Play Store) complement developer-provided profiles
 *
 * ⚠️ Without Baseline Profiles:
 *   First launch → ART interprets bytecode → JIT compiles hot methods over time
 *   → First run is noticeably slower than subsequent runs
 *
 * 💡 Interview Tip: Baseline Profiles are a Google-created technology.
 *    Demonstrating hands-on knowledge (Macrobenchmark, BaselineProfileRule,
 *    CI integration) is a strong signal for SDE-2 performance questions.
 */

// Step 1: Add the Macrobenchmark module (build.gradle.kts)
/*
// :benchmark module build.gradle.kts
plugins {
    id("com.android.test")
    id("org.jetbrains.kotlin.android")
}

android {
    namespace = "com.example.benchmark"
    targetProjectPath = ":app"
    experimentalProperties["android.experimental.self-instrumenting"] = true
}

dependencies {
    implementation("androidx.benchmark:benchmark-macro-junit4:1.2.0")
    implementation("androidx.test.ext:junit:1.1.5")
    implementation("androidx.test.uiautomator:uiautomator:2.2.0")
}
*/
