    Full categorized list: [19_googleyness_questions.md](19_googleyness_questions.md) (96 questions).

    What Google Looks For at L4

  • Ownership — you drive outcomes, not just tasks
  • Technical judgment — trade-offs, not buzzwords
  • Collaboration — influence peers and XFN partners
  • User focus — tie work to user or business impact
  • Growth mindset — learn from failure, seek feedback
  • Humility — credit the team, own your mistakes

    • Avoid: blaming others, vague answers ("we improved things"), stories where you were a passive observer.

    AI Answer-Practice Prompt (Optional)

    Paste into Claude/ChatGPT/Gemini, fill in your profile and stories, then practice one question at a time.

    You are an interview coach. I am interviewing for Google Android SDE-2 (L4) with 4+ years of experience.

When I give you a behavioral question, return a STAR answer using ONLY my context below.
- 2–3 minutes when spoken aloud
- Use "I" for my actions
- Include metrics where available
- End with a learning and one trade-off
- If the question is reflective, ground it in a real experience from my context

### MY PROFILE
[Name, years of experience, companies, why Google, career goals]

### MY STORIES (fill in 6–8)
STORY 1 — [Title]: Situation, Task, Actions, Result, Learning
STORY 2 — ...
...

Now I'll give you one question at a time.

    Prep Checklist

    ☐ Write 6–8 STAR stories with metrics

    ☐ Practice each story out loud (2–3 min each)

    ☐ Map stories to question categories in [19_googleyness_questions.md](19_googleyness_questions.md)

    ☐ Prepare "Why Google?" and "Why now?" with specifics (product, scale, team)

    ☐ Have one failure story you're comfortable sharing

    ☐ Rehearse with a friend or AI — avoid sounding scripted

    Sample STAR Answers (4 Exemplars)

    Use these as templates — replace with your own projects, metrics, and names.

    Exemplar 1: Self-Initiated Impact

    Question: "Tell me about a time you identified a problem and fixed it without being asked."

    Hook: I drove a crash-rate reduction that nobody had prioritized — because users were paying for it every day.

    Situation & Task: On our Android app, crash-free sessions had dropped below our internal bar while the team was heads-down on a feature launch. There was no named owner for the investigation. I owned the networking layer and felt responsible for stability, so I treated it as a product risk, not a side quest.

    Actions: I spent two evenings in Crashlytics and found ~60% of crashes clustered around WebSocket reconnect races and brittle JSON parsing on the realtime path. I wrote a one-pager with impact estimates and proposed sequencing, then shared it with my manager before writing code — so it was a business decision, not just my preference. After alignment, I added defensive parsing with sealed Result types, exponential backoff on reconnect, and tied coroutine scopes to lifecycle so failures stopped compounding. I also added a dashboard so we could catch regressions early.

    Result: Crash rate dropped ~40% over three weeks. Crash-free sessions went back above 99.5%. We avoided two emergency hotfix releases during the feature launch.

    Trade-off: I borrowed a week from roadmap work; I made that explicit upfront when negotiating priority.

    Learning: Stability problems need the same visibility as features — a short written proposal gets buy-in faster than fixing things quietly.

    Exemplar 2: Mentoring / Growing Others

    Question: "Tell me about a time you helped a teammate who was struggling."

    Hook: I ramped a junior engineer from zero Android experience to independently owning a core SDK feature in about two months.

    Situation & Task: A new hire joined our mobile SDK team straight out of college. Their early PRs needed many revision rounds, and I was already carrying review load for a sensitive area. My job was to grow them without trading away my own velocity or our quality bar.

    Actions: I started with pair programming on small, scoped bug fixes so they could see how I reasoned about lifecycle and state. When I noticed I was giving line-level fixes, I deliberately switched to guiding questions — where should state live, what breaks on rotation — so they had to defend their model. I reviewed approach before nitpicking diffs, which cost more time upfront but cut churn later. I only expanded task scope after they could explain trade-offs in our weekly 1:1.

    Result: Within two months they independently owned wireframe traversal for session replay — a core SDK capability. Review cycles shortened and their PRs needed fewer structural rewrites.

    Trade-off: Coaching this way is slower than fixing files yourself short-term.

    Learning: Structured autonomy with a rising bar beats both hand-holding and sink-or-swim.

    Exemplar 3: Conflict / Disagreement

    Question: "Tell me about a time you disagreed with your manager. How did you handle it?"

    Hook: I pushed back on shipping a caching layer that would have saved us two weeks but created long-term maintenance pain.

    Situation & Task: We needed offline support for a feed feature on a tight deadline. My manager proposed a custom in-memory cache with manual invalidation. I believed Room as single source of truth was the right long-term architecture, but I knew "just use Room" without data would sound like scope creep.

    Actions: Instead of arguing in the meeting, I spent one evening building a thin spike: Room + Flow observing the same API with a two-day scope estimate vs the cache approach. I documented trade-offs in a short comparison — complexity, testability, process-death behavior, and migration cost. In the next sync I presented both options with timelines and risks, and recommended Room with a reduced v1 scope (cache last 50 items only). I explicitly said I could deliver the cache approach if we accepted the tech-debt ticket I attached.

    Result: We shipped the Room-based v1 one week later than the cache shortcut would have taken, but avoided a rewrite when pagination and search were added the next quarter. My manager later used that doc as a template for similar decisions.

    Trade-off: I accepted a one-week delay on the initial ship date.

    Learning: Disagreement lands better with a prototype and written trade-offs than with architecture opinions alone.

    Exemplar 4: Failure / Mistake

    Question: "Tell me about a time you failed. What did you learn?"

    Hook: I shipped a performance "optimization" that actually regressed cold start — and I caught it because I had skipped measurement.

    Situation & Task: I was optimizing app startup and moved several initializations into a lazy singleton to defer work off the critical path. I was confident from code reading that it would help — and I merged without running Macrobenchmark on a release build.

    Actions: Two days after release, our startup P95 regressed in Play Console vitals. I rolled back within hours, then profiled properly with Macrobenchmark and Perfetto. The lazy singleton was triggering synchronized initialization on the first user interaction — which happened during startup for our default flow — so we paid the cost anyway, plus extra indirection. I wrote a postmortem for the team, added a CI Macrobenchmark gate for cold start, and changed our review checklist to require before/after numbers for perf changes.

    Result: After the rollback and a measured fix (Baseline Profile + deferred non-critical init), cold start improved ~18% with data to prove it. The CI gate caught one similar regression in the next month.

    Trade-off: The rollback delayed a feature that depended on the same release train by one week.

    Learning: Performance work without measurement is guesswork — I now treat benchmarks like tests, not optional polish.

    See also: [16_interview_questions.md](16_interview_questions.md) for the technical domain round.

    19 Googleyness Questions

    19_googleyness_questions.md · Markdown guide

    Category 11: Reflective / Abstract Questions

    These are a distinctive Google pattern -- rather than asking "tell me about a time when...",

    the interviewer asks you to reflect on an abstract concept. The recommended approach is to

    ground your answer in a specific past experience anyway.

  • 93. "What do you think about setting goals?"

    • - Source: Interviewing.io (ex-Google interviewer guide, noted as a characteristic Google reflective question)

  • 94. "If you were going to plan an offsite for the team, how would you do it?"

    • - Source: Interviewing.io (ex-Google interviewer guide)

  • 95. "We consider research to be the backbone of what we do at Google. Do you think it's important to conduct research before beginning a project or would you rather jump right in?"

    • - Source: MockQuestions.com

  • 96. "How do you keep up-to-date on new trends and changes in your line of work?"

    • - Source: MockQuestions.com

    Key Patterns Observed

    Pattern 1: Google Prefers "Tell Me About a Time" Format

    The overwhelming majority of G&L questions are situation-based, asking for specific past

    experiences. Generic hypothetical answers score poorly.

    Pattern 2: Google Also Asks Reflective Questions

    Unlike Amazon (pure STAR), Google sometimes asks open-ended reflective questions like

    "What do you think about X?" The recommended strategy (per interviewing.io) is to convert

    these into situational answers grounded in real experience.

    Pattern 3: The Five Core Signals

    Based on InterviewBit's definition and multiple sources, Google evaluates Googleyness on:

  • Doing the right thing even when it is hard
  • Comfort with ambiguity and imperfect information
  • Genuine collaboration over individual heroics
  • Intellectual humility (admitting what you do not know)
  • Continuous learning and curiosity

    • Pattern 4: Leadership = Influence Without Authority

    For IC (individual contributor) roles like L4 SWE, leadership is NOT about managing people.

    It is about:

  • Influencing technical decisions through data and empathy
  • Mentoring and growing others
  • Taking ownership of problems nobody assigned you
  • Driving initiatives from idea to completion

    • Pattern 5: Behavioral Round Is De-Emphasized but Still Scored

    Per interviewing.io's ex-Google interviewer guide, the behavioral round is the "least

    important" of the four interview loops and is sometimes skipped entirely. However, when

    it does happen, it carries equal weight in the hiring committee packet. A bad G&L score

    can sink an otherwise strong candidate.

    Pattern 6: Most Frequently Asked Topics (by frequency across sources)

  • 1. Conflict/Disagreement -- appeared in every source
  • 2. Failure/Mistakes -- appeared in every source
  • 3. Leadership without authority -- appeared in every source
  • 4. Collaboration/Cross-functional work -- appeared in every source
  • 5. Navigating ambiguity -- appeared in most sources
  • 6. Mentoring/Growing others -- appeared in most sources
  • 7. Initiative/Going above and beyond -- appeared in most sources
  • 8. Receiving and giving feedback -- appeared in several sources
  • 9. Why Google? -- universally asked as opener or closer

    • What Google Is NOT Looking For (Anti-Patterns from Sources)

  • Blaming others for failures
  • Taking all credit on team projects
  • Claiming you have never failed
  • Generic answers without specific examples
  • Using "we" for everything (interviewer cannot assess YOUR contribution)
  • Badmouthing previous employers
  • Not showing growth or learning from experiences
  • Rehearsed-sounding robotic answers

    • 1. Target Sum (LC 494)

    Problem: Assign + or - to each num. Count ways to reach target.

    Key Insight

    At each index, two choices: add or subtract. Brute force = 2^n paths.

    Subset-sum conversion:

  • Let P = sum of positives, N = sum of negatives
  • P - N = target and P + N = totalSum
  • → P = (totalSum + target) / 2
  • Reduces to: count subsets with sum = S

    • 1. Recursion

    public int findTargetSumWays(int[] nums, int target) {
    return solve(nums, 0, target);
}

private int solve(int[] nums, int idx, int remaining) {
    if (idx == nums.length) return remaining == 0 ? 1 : 0;
    return solve(nums, idx + 1, remaining - nums[idx])   // assign +
         + solve(nums, idx + 1, remaining + nums[idx]);  // assign -
}
  • TC: O(2^n) — binary tree of calls
  • SC: O(n) — call stack depth

    • 2. Memoization (Top-Down)

    Map<String, Integer> memo = new HashMap<>();

public int findTargetSumWays(int[] nums, int target) {
    memo.clear();
    return solve(nums, 0, target);
}

private int solve(int[] nums, int idx, int remaining) {
    String key = idx + "," + remaining;
    if (memo.containsKey(key)) return memo.get(key);
    if (idx == nums.length) return remaining == 0 ? 1 : 0;

    int result = solve(nums, idx + 1, remaining - nums[idx])
               + solve(nums, idx + 1, remaining + nums[idx]);
    memo.put(key, result);
    return result;
}
  • Intuition: Same recursion, cache (idx, remaining) to avoid recomputation.
  • TC: O(n × totalSum)
  • SC: O(n × totalSum) — memo + stack

    • 3. Tabulation (Bottom-Up)

    public int findTargetSumWays(int[] nums, int target) {
    int totalSum = 0;
    for (int n : nums) totalSum += n;

    if (Math.abs(target) > totalSum || (totalSum + target) % 2 != 0) return 0;

    int S = (totalSum + target) / 2;
    int n = nums.length;
    int[][] dp = new int[n + 1][S + 1];
    dp[0][0] = 1; // 0 elements, sum=0 → 1 way

    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j <= S; j++) {
            dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j];                                    // skip num
            if (j >= nums[i-1]) dp[i][j] += dp[i-1][j - nums[i-1]]; // take num
        }
    }
    return dp[n][S];
}
  • Intuition: dp[i][j] = ways to pick subset from first i nums summing to j.
  • TC: O(n × S)
  • SC: O(n × S)

    • 4. Space Optimization

    public int findTargetSumWays(int[] nums, int target) {
    int totalSum = 0;
    for (int n : nums) totalSum += n;

    if (Math.abs(target) > totalSum || (totalSum + target) % 2 != 0) return 0;

    int S = (totalSum + target) / 2;
    int[] dp = new int[S + 1];
    dp[0] = 1;

    for (int num : nums) {
        for (int j = S; j >= num; j--) { // right-to-left: read i-1 values (0/1 knapsack)
            dp[j] += dp[j - num];
        }
    }
    return dp[S];
}
  • Intuition: Only previous row needed → collapse to 1D. Traverse right-to-left so we read i-1 values, not i.
  • TC: O(n × S)
  • SC: O(S)

    • Edge Cases

  • (totalSum + target) % 2 != 0 → no valid split, return 0
  • |target| > totalSum → impossible, return 0
  • Zeros in array → handled correctly

    • 2. Coin Change 2 (LC 518)

    Problem: Given coins of infinite supply and an amount, return number of combinations to make up that amount.

    Key Insight

    Unbounded knapsack — each coin can be used unlimited times.

  • At each coin, two choices: skip it or use it (and stay at same coin index).
  • Unlike 0/1 knapsack (move to next item after taking), here we stay because coin is reusable.
  • Order doesn't matter → combinations, not permutations (outer loop = items, not amount).

    • 1. Recursion

    public int change(int amount, int[] coins) {
    return solve(coins, 0, amount);
}

private int solve(int[] coins, int idx, int remaining) {
    if (remaining == 0) return 1;                     // exact match
    if (idx == coins.length || remaining < 0) return 0;

    // skip current coin OR use current coin (stay at idx — unbounded)
    return solve(coins, idx + 1, remaining)
         + solve(coins, idx, remaining - coins[idx]);
}
  • TC: O(2^amount) — exponential
  • SC: O(amount) — call stack depth

    • 2. Memoization (Top-Down)

    int[][] memo;

public int change(int amount, int[] coins) {
    memo = new int[coins.length][amount + 1];
    for (int[] row : memo) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
    return solve(coins, 0, amount);
}

private int solve(int[] coins, int idx, int remaining) {
    if (remaining == 0) return 1;
    if (idx == coins.length || remaining < 0) return 0;
    if (memo[idx][remaining] != -1) return memo[idx][remaining];

    memo[idx][remaining] = solve(coins, idx + 1, remaining)
                         + solve(coins, idx, remaining - coins[idx]);
    return memo[idx][remaining];
}
  • Intuition: Cache (idx, remaining). State space = coins × amount unique subproblems.
  • TC: O(n × amount)
  • SC: O(n × amount) — memo + stack

    • 3. Tabulation (Bottom-Up)

    public int change(int amount, int[] coins) {
    int n = coins.length;
    int[][] dp = new int[n + 1][amount + 1];

    // base case: 0 amount → 1 way (use no coins) for all rows
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) dp[i][0] = 1;

    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j <= amount; j++) {
            dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j];                                    // skip coin i
            if (j >= coins[i-1]) dp[i][j] += dp[i][j - coins[i-1]]; // use coin i (same row = reuse!)
        }
    }
    return dp[n][amount];
}
  • Intuition: dp[i][j] = combinations using first i coins for amount j.

    • Key difference from 0/1 knapsack: use dp[i][j - coins[i-1]] (same row) not dp[i-1][...] (prev row) — allows reuse.

  • TC: O(n × amount)
  • SC: O(n × amount)

    • 4. Space Optimization

    public int change(int amount, int[] coins) {
    int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];
    dp[0] = 1;

    for (int coin : coins) {
        for (int j = coin; j <= amount; j++) { // left-to-right: reads updated values → models reuse
            dp[j] += dp[j - coin];
        }
    }
    return dp[amount];
}
  • Intuition: 1D array. Traverse left-to-right (opposite of 0/1 knapsack!) so dp[j - coin] is already updated for current coin → models unbounded reuse.
  • TC: O(n × amount)
  • SC: O(amount)

    • Key Distinction from 0/1 Knapsack

    0/1 KnapsackUnbounded (Coin Change 2)
    TraversalRight → LeftLeft → Right
    ReuseNoYes
    Takes fromPrevious rowCurrent row

    Edge Cases

  • amount == 0 → return 1 (one way: use nothing)
  • Coin larger than amount → skip naturally
  • No valid combo → return 0

    • 3. Climbing Stairs (LC 70)

    Problem: n steps, can climb 1 or 2 at a time. How many distinct ways?

    Key Insight

    Same as Fibonacci: ways(n) = ways(n-1) + ways(n-2). Classic 1D DP intro.

    Optimal (Space-Optimized)

    fun climbStairs(n: Int): Int {
    if (n <= 2) return n
    var prev2 = 1  // ways(1)
    var prev1 = 2  // ways(2)
    for (i in 3..n) {
        val curr = prev1 + prev2
        prev2 = prev1
        prev1 = curr
    }
    return prev1
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(1)
  • Edge cases: n = 1 → 1, n = 2 → 2

    • 4. Longest Increasing Subsequence (LC 300)

    Problem: Length of longest strictly increasing subsequence (not necessarily contiguous).

    Key Insight

    Patience sorting / binary search: Maintain tails[i] = smallest tail of an LIS of length i+1.

    For each num, binary search where to place it. O(n log n).

    Optimal

    fun lengthOfLIS(nums: IntArray): Int {
    val tails = IntArray(nums.size)
    var size = 0
    for (num in nums) {
        var lo = 0
        var hi = size
        while (lo < hi) {
            val mid = (lo + hi) / 2
            if (tails[mid] < num) lo = mid + 1 else hi = mid
        }
        tails[lo] = num
        if (lo == size) size++
    }
    return size
}
  • TC: O(n log n) | SC: O(n)
  • Follow-up: DP O(n²) is easier to explain in interviews; mention both.

    • 5. Two Sum (LC 1)

    Problem: Return indices of two numbers that add to target.

    Key Insight

    One pass with HashMap: for each num, check if target - num exists.

    fun twoSum(nums: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = HashMap<Int, Int>()
    for (i in nums.indices) {
        val complement = target - nums[i]
        seen[complement]?.let { return intArrayOf(it, i) }
        seen[nums[i]] = i
    }
    return intArrayOf()
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(n)
  • Edge cases: Exactly one solution guaranteed; don't reuse same element

    • 6. Product of Array Except Self (LC 238)

    Problem: Return array where output[i] = product of all elements except nums[i]. No division.

    Key Insight

    Prefix products left-to-right, then suffix products right-to-left in one output array.

    fun productExceptSelf(nums: IntArray): IntArray {
    val n = nums.size
    val result = IntArray(n) { 1 }
    var prefix = 1
    for (i in 0 until n) {
        result[i] = prefix
        prefix *= nums[i]
    }
    var suffix = 1
    for (i in n - 1 downTo 0) {
        result[i] *= suffix
        suffix *= nums[i]
    }
    return result
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(1) excluding output
  • Follow-up: Handle zeros — zero in array makes most outputs zero

    • 7. Merge Intervals (LC 56)

    Problem: Merge overlapping intervals.

    Key Insight

    Sort by start, then merge if current overlaps with last merged.

    fun merge(intervals: Array<IntArray>): Array<IntArray> {
    if (intervals.isEmpty()) return intervals
    intervals.sortBy { it[0] }
    val merged = mutableListOf<IntArray>()
    merged.add(intervals[0])
    for (i in 1 until intervals.size) {
        val last = merged.last()
        if (intervals[i][0] <= last[1]) {
            last[1] = maxOf(last[1], intervals[i][1])
        } else {
            merged.add(intervals[i])
        }
    }
    return merged.toTypedArray()
}
  • TC: O(n log n) | SC: O(n)
  • Edge cases: Single interval, all overlapping, no overlap

    • 8. Longest Substring Without Repeating (LC 3)

    Problem: Length of longest substring without repeating characters.

    Key Insight

    Sliding window + HashMap of last seen index. Shrink left when duplicate found.

    fun lengthOfLongestSubstring(s: String): Int {
    val lastSeen = HashMap<Char, Int>()
    var maxLen = 0
    var left = 0
    for (right in s.indices) {
        val c = s[right]
        if (c in lastSeen && lastSeen[c]!! >= left) {
            left = lastSeen[c]!! + 1
        }
        lastSeen[c] = right
        maxLen = maxOf(maxLen, right - left + 1)
    }
    return maxLen
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(min(n, charset))
  • Pattern: Variable-size sliding window

    • 9. Sliding Window Maximum (LC 239)

    Problem: Max in each sliding window of size k.

    Key Insight

    Monotonic deque stores indices in decreasing value order. Front = max for current window.

    fun maxSlidingWindow(nums: IntArray, k: Int): IntArray {
    val deque = ArrayDeque<Int>()
    val result = IntArray(nums.size - k + 1)
    for (i in nums.indices) {
        while (deque.isNotEmpty() && deque.first() < i - k + 1) deque.removeFirst()
        while (deque.isNotEmpty() && nums[deque.last()] < nums[i]) deque.removeLast()
        deque.addLast(i)
        if (i >= k - 1) result[i - k + 1] = nums[deque.first()]
    }
    return result
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(k)
  • Hard variant: Know this for Google — tests deque + window reasoning

    • 10. Binary Tree Level Order Traversal (LC 102)

    Problem: Return level-order traversal (BFS).

    fun levelOrder(root: TreeNode?): List<List<Int>> {
    if (root == null) return emptyList()
    val result = mutableListOf<List<Int>>()
    val queue = ArrayDeque<TreeNode>()
    queue.add(root)
    while (queue.isNotEmpty()) {
        val level = mutableListOf<Int>()
        repeat(queue.size) {
            val node = queue.removeFirst()
            level.add(node.`val`)
            node.left?.let { queue.add(it) }
            node.right?.let { queue.add(it) }
        }
        result.add(level)
    }
    return result
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(n)
  • Follow-up: Zigzag level order (LC 103) — reverse every other level

    • 11. Validate Binary Search Tree (LC 98)

    Problem: Determine if a binary tree is a valid BST.

    Key Insight

    Pass valid (min, max) range down recursively. Not enough to check only parent-child.

    fun isValidBST(root: TreeNode?): Boolean {
    fun validate(node: TreeNode?, min: Long, max: Long): Boolean {
        if (node == null) return true
        if (node.`val`.toLong() <= min || node.`val`.toLong() >= max) return false
        return validate(node.left, min, node.`val`.toLong()) &&
               validate(node.right, node.`val`.toLong(), max)
    }
    return validate(root, Long.MIN_VALUE, Long.MAX_VALUE)
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(h)
  • Gotcha: Use Long — Int.MIN_VALUE node breaks val < min check

    • 12. Lowest Common Ancestor of BST (LC 236)

    Problem: Find LCA of two nodes in a BST.

    Key Insight

    If both values smaller → go left. Both larger → go right. Otherwise current node is LCA.

    fun lowestCommonAncestor(root: TreeNode?, p: TreeNode?, q: TreeNode?): TreeNode? {
    var curr = root
    while (curr != null) {
        when {
            p!!.`val` < curr.`val` && q!!.`val` < curr.`val` -> curr = curr.left
            p.`val` > curr.`val` && q.`val` > curr.`val` -> curr = curr.right
            else -> return curr
        }
    }
    return null
}
  • TC: O(h) | SC: O(1)
  • General tree variant (LC 236): Recursive post-order — different problem, know both

    • 13. Number of Islands (LC 200)

    Problem: Count connected components of '1' in a 2D grid.

    Key Insight

    DFS or BFS from each unvisited '1', mark visited. Increment count per new DFS start.

    fun numIslands(grid: Array<CharArray>): Int {
    if (grid.isEmpty()) return 0
    val rows = grid.size
    val cols = grid[0].size
    var count = 0

    fun dfs(r: Int, c: Int) {
        if (r !in 0 until rows || c !in 0 until cols || grid[r][c] != '1') return
        grid[r][c] = '0'  // mark visited
        dfs(r + 1, c); dfs(r - 1, c); dfs(r, c + 1); dfs(r, c - 1)
    }

    for (r in 0 until rows) {
        for (c in 0 until cols) {
            if (grid[r][c] == '1') {
                count++
                dfs(r, c)
            }
        }
    }
    return count
}
  • TC: O(rows × cols) | SC: O(rows × cols) worst-case recursion stack
  • Variants: Max area of island (LC 695), surrounded regions (LC 130)

    • 14. Course Schedule (LC 207)

    Problem: Can you finish all courses? prerequisites[i] = [a, b] means take b before a.

    Key Insight

    Topological sort — detect cycle in directed graph. Kahn's algorithm (BFS indegree) or DFS with 3-color.

    fun canFinish(numCourses: Int, prerequisites: Array<IntArray>): Boolean {
    val adj = Array(numCourses) { mutableListOf<Int>() }
    val indegree = IntArray(numCourses)
    for ((course, prereq) in prerequisites) {
        adj[prereq].add(course)
        indegree[course]++
    }
    val queue = ArrayDeque<Int>()
    for (i in 0 until numCourses) {
        if (indegree[i] == 0) queue.add(i)
    }
    var visited = 0
    while (queue.isNotEmpty()) {
        val node = queue.removeFirst()
        visited++
        for (next in adj[node]) {
            if (--indegree[next] == 0) queue.add(next)
        }
    }
    return visited == numCourses
}
  • TC: O(V + E) | SC: O(V + E)
  • Follow-up: Return ordering (LC 210) — append to result in Kahn's loop

    • 15. Binary Search (LC 704)

    Problem: Find target in sorted array, return index or -1.

    Key Insight

    Classic template: lo, hi, mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2. Narrow based on comparison.

    fun search(nums: IntArray, target: Int): Int {
    var lo = 0
    var hi = nums.size - 1
    while (lo <= hi) {
        val mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2
        when {
            nums[mid] == target -> return mid
            nums[mid] < target -> lo = mid + 1
            else -> hi = mid - 1
        }
    }
    return -1
}
  • TC: O(log n) | SC: O(1)
  • Template: Use while (lo <= hi) for exact match; while (lo < hi) for boundary problems

    • 16. Search in Rotated Sorted Array (LC 33)

    Problem: Sorted array rotated at unknown pivot. Search in O(log n).

    Key Insight

    One half is always sorted. Check which half contains target.

    fun search(nums: IntArray, target: Int): Int {
    var lo = 0
    var hi = nums.size - 1
    while (lo <= hi) {
        val mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2
        if (nums[mid] == target) return mid
        if (nums[lo] <= nums[mid]) {
            if (target in nums[lo]..<nums[mid]) hi = mid - 1 else lo = mid + 1
        } else {
            if (target in nums[mid + 1]..nums[hi]) lo = mid + 1 else hi = mid - 1
        }
    }
    return -1
}
  • TC: O(log n) | SC: O(1)
  • Follow-up: With duplicates (LC 81) — worst case O(n) when all same

    • 17. Kth Largest Element (LC 215)

    Problem: Find the kth largest element in unsorted array.

    Key Insight

    Min-heap of size k — root is kth largest. Quickselect is O(n) average but heap is easier under interview pressure.

    fun findKthLargest(nums: IntArray, k: Int): Int {
    val heap = PriorityQueue<Int>()  // min-heap by default in Java PQ
    for (num in nums) {
        heap.offer(num)
        if (heap.size > k) heap.poll()
    }
    return heap.peek()
}
  • TC: O(n log k) | SC: O(k)
  • Follow-up: Quickselect O(n) average — mention if asked about optimization

    • 18. Top K Frequent Elements (LC 347)

    Problem: Return k most frequent elements.

    Key Insight

    Count with HashMap, then min-heap of size k on frequency. Bucket sort is O(n) if range is bounded.

    fun topKFrequent(nums: IntArray, k: Int): IntArray {
    val freq = nums.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
    val heap = PriorityQueue<Pair<Int, Int>>(compareBy { it.second })  // min-heap by freq
    for ((num, count) in freq) {
        heap.offer(num to count)
        if (heap.size > k) heap.poll()
    }
    return heap.map { it.first }.toIntArray()
}
  • TC: O(n log k) | SC: O(n)
  • Google favorite: Combines hash map + heap — very common phone screen pattern

    • 19. Subsets (LC 78)

    Problem: Return all subsets (power set) of distinct integers.

    Key Insight

    Backtracking: at each index, choose include or exclude. Or iterative — double result set for each new element.

    fun subsets(nums: IntArray): List<List<Int>> {
    val result = mutableListOf<List<Int>>()
    fun backtrack(start: Int, path: MutableList<Int>) {
        result.add(path.toList())
        for (i in start until nums.size) {
            path.add(nums[i])
            backtrack(i + 1, path)
            path.removeAt(path.lastIndex)
        }
    }
    backtrack(0, mutableListOf())
    return result
}
  • TC: O(n × 2^n) | SC: O(n) recursion depth
  • Pattern: Include/exclude branching — base for many backtracking problems

    • 20. Permutations (LC 46)

    Problem: Return all permutations of distinct integers.

    Key Insight

    Backtracking with used boolean array. Swap-based approach also works (LC 47 with duplicates).

    fun permute(nums: IntArray): List<List<Int>> {
    val result = mutableListOf<List<Int>>()
    val used = BooleanArray(nums.size)
    fun backtrack(path: MutableList<Int>) {
        if (path.size == nums.size) {
            result.add(path.toList())
            return
        }
        for (i in nums.indices) {
            if (used[i]) continue
            used[i] = true
            path.add(nums[i])
            backtrack(path)
            path.removeAt(path.lastIndex)
            used[i] = false
        }
    }
    backtrack(mutableListOf())
    return result
}
  • TC: O(n × n!) | SC: O(n)
  • Follow-up: Permutations II (LC 47) — sort + skip duplicates at same level

    • 21. Word Search (LC 79)

    Problem: Find if word exists in 2D board (adjacent cells, no reuse).

    Key Insight

    DFS backtracking with in-place marking (#) to avoid extra visited array.

    fun exist(board: Array<CharArray>, word: String): Boolean {
    val rows = board.size
    val cols = board[0].size
    fun dfs(r: Int, c: Int, idx: Int): Boolean {
        if (idx == word.length) return true
        if (r !in 0 until rows || c !in 0 until cols || board[r][c] != word[idx]) return false
        val temp = board[r][c]
        board[r][c] = '#'
        val found = dfs(r + 1, c, idx + 1) || dfs(r - 1, c, idx + 1) ||
                    dfs(r, c + 1, idx + 1) || dfs(r, c - 1, idx + 1)
        board[r][c] = temp
        return found
    }
    for (r in 0 until rows) {
        for (c in 0 until cols) {
            if (dfs(r, c, 0)) return true
        }
    }
    return false
}
  • TC: O(rows × cols × 4^L) | SC: O(L) stack
  • Pattern: Grid DFS + undo — same as islands with path constraint

    • 22. Meeting Rooms II (LC 253)

    Problem: Minimum conference rooms needed for overlapping intervals.

    Key Insight

    Sort by start time. Min-heap tracks end times of ongoing meetings. If earliest end ≤ new start, reuse room.

    fun minMeetingRooms(intervals: Array<IntArray>): Int {
    if (intervals.isEmpty()) return 0
    intervals.sortBy { it[0] }
    val heap = PriorityQueue<Int>()  // end times
    heap.offer(intervals[0][1])
    for (i in 1 until intervals.size) {
        if (heap.peek() <= intervals[i][0]) heap.poll()
        heap.offer(intervals[i][1])
    }
    return heap.size
}
  • TC: O(n log n) | SC: O(n)
  • Alternative: Chronological ordering of all start/end events — same complexity, different intuition

    • 23. Reverse Linked List (LC 206)

    Problem: Reverse a singly linked list in-place.

    Key Insight

    Three pointers: prev, curr, next. Iterative is O(1) space; recursive is O(n) stack.

    fun reverseList(head: ListNode?): ListNode? {
    var prev: ListNode? = null
    var curr = head
    while (curr != null) {
        val next = curr.next
        curr.next = prev
        prev = curr
        curr = next
    }
    return prev
}
  • TC: O(n) | SC: O(1)
  • Follow-ups: Reverse between m-n (LC 92), palindrome linked list (LC 234)

    • 24. Merge Two Sorted Lists (LC 21)

    Problem: Merge two sorted linked lists into one sorted list.

    Key Insight

    Dummy head + tail pointer. Compare heads, attach smaller, advance.

    fun mergeTwoLists(l1: ListNode?, l2: ListNode?): ListNode? {
    val dummy = ListNode(0)
    var tail = dummy
    var a = l1
    var b = l2
    while (a != null && b != null) {
        if (a.`val` <= b.`val`) {
            tail.next = a
            a = a.next
        } else {
            tail.next = b
            b = b.next
        }
        tail = tail.next!!
    }
    tail.next = a ?: b
    return dummy.next
}
  • TC: O(n + m) | SC: O(1)
  • Pattern: Dummy node simplifies head insertion — use for many linked list problems

    • 25. LRU Cache (LC 146)

    Problem: Design LRU cache with get and put in O(1).

    Key Insight

    HashMap for O(1) lookup + doubly linked list for O(1) eviction of least recently used.

    On access: move node to front. On capacity: remove tail.

    class LRUCache(capacity: Int) {
    private val cap = capacity
    private val map = HashMap<Int, Node>()
    private val head = Node(0, 0)
    private val tail = Node(0, 0)

    init {
        head.next = tail
        tail.prev = head
    }

    private class Node(val key: Int, var value: Int) {
        var prev: Node? = null
        var next: Node? = null
    }

    fun get(key: Int): Int {
        val node = map[key] ?: return -1
        moveToFront(node)
        return node.value
    }

    fun put(key: Int, value: Int) {
        if (map.containsKey(key)) {
            map[key]!!.value = value
            moveToFront(map[key]!!)
            return
        }
        if (map.size == cap) {
            val lru = tail.prev!!
            remove(lru)
            map.remove(lru.key)
        }
        val node = Node(key, value)
        map[key] = node
        insertAfterHead(node)
    }

    private fun moveToFront(node: Node) {
        remove(node)
        insertAfterHead(node)
    }

    private fun remove(node: Node) {
        node.prev?.next = node.next
        node.next?.prev = node.prev
    }

    private fun insertAfterHead(node: Node) {
        node.next = head.next
        node.prev = head
        head.next?.prev = node
        head.next = node
    }
}
  • TC: O(1) per operation | SC: O(capacity)
  • Google favorite: Tests data structure design — explain why HashMap + DLL, not HashMap alone

    • 26. Serialize and Deserialize Binary Tree (LC 297)

    Problem: Serialize binary tree to string and deserialize back.

    Key Insight

    Preorder with null markers — unambiguous reconstruction. Use # for null.

    class Codec {
    fun serialize(root: TreeNode?): String {
        if (root == null) return "#"
        return "${root.`val`},${serialize(root.left)},${serialize(root.right)}"
    }

    fun deserialize(data: String): TreeNode? {
        val tokens = data.split(",").toMutableList()
        return build(tokens)
    }

    private fun build(tokens: MutableList<String>): TreeNode? {
        if (tokens.isEmpty()) return null
        val first = tokens.removeAt(0)
        if (first == "#") return null
        val node = TreeNode(first.toInt())
        node.left = build(tokens)
        node.right = build(tokens)
        return node
    }
}
  • TC: O(n) serialize + deserialize | SC: O(n)
  • Follow-up: BFS level-order serialization — know both; preorder+null is simpler to code

    • 27. Median of Two Sorted Arrays (LC 4)

    Problem: Find median of two sorted arrays in O(log(m+n)).

    Key Insight

    Binary search on the shorter array's partition. Partition both arrays so left halves form the lower half of merged array. Median depends on max(left) and min(right).

    fun findMedianSortedArrays(nums1: IntArray, nums2: IntArray): Double {
    if (nums1.size > nums2.size) return findMedianSortedArrays(nums2, nums1)
    val m = nums1.size
    val n = nums2.size
    var lo = 0
    var hi = m
    while (lo <= hi) {
        val i = (lo + hi) / 2
        val j = (m + n + 1) / 2 - i
        val maxLeft1 = if (i == 0) Int.MIN_VALUE else nums1[i - 1]
        val minRight1 = if (i == m) Int.MAX_VALUE else nums1[i]
        val maxLeft2 = if (j == 0) Int.MIN_VALUE else nums2[j - 1]
        val minRight2 = if (j == n) Int.MAX_VALUE else nums2[j]
        if (maxLeft1 <= minRight2 && maxLeft2 <= minRight1) {
            return if ((m + n) % 2 == 1) {
                maxOf(maxLeft1, maxLeft2).toDouble()
            } else {
                (maxOf(maxLeft1, maxLeft2) + minOf(minRight1, minRight2)) / 2.0
            }
        } else if (maxLeft1 > minRight2) {
            hi = i - 1
        } else {
            lo = i + 1
        }
    }
    return 0.0
}
  • TC: O(log(min(m,n))) | SC: O(1)
  • Interview tip: Hard problem — explain intuition (partitioning merged array) even if code is shaky. Merge approach O(m+n) is acceptable warm-up.

    Study Order for Google Coding Round

  • 1. Arrays + Hash Maps (5–7) — most phone screens start here
  • 2. Sliding Window (8–9) — very common at Google
  • 3. Binary Search (15–16) — know the template cold
  • 4. Trees (10–12) — BFS/DFS + BST property
  • 5. Graphs (13–14) — islands + topological sort
  • 6. Heaps (17–18) — top K / kth element pattern
  • 7. Backtracking (19–21) — subsets → permutations → grid DFS
  • 8. Linked Lists (23–24) — reverse, merge, dummy head pattern
  • 9. DP (1–4) — go deep on 1–2 patterns, not every variant
  • 10. Hard (25–27) — LRU Cache, Serialize Tree, Median (onsite-level)

    Checklist:

    ☐ List ANR triggers and Play Console thresholds

    ☐ Explain Baseline Profiles and when to use Macrobenchmark

    ☐ Decision tree: WorkManager vs FGS vs AlarmManager vs Coroutines

    ☐ What survives process death vs config change

    Day 5 — System Design + Testing + Security

    Goal: One full system design whiteboard; testing and security depth.

    BlockTimeActivity
    Read90 min[15_system_design_android.kt](15_system_design_android.kt), [11_testing.kt](11_testing.kt), [12_security.kt](12_security.kt)
    Practice90 minWhiteboard offline-first notes app OR chat app (15); Tier 2: Q14, Q16–Q17
    Review30 minlearn.html — Topics 11–12, 15

    Checklist:

    ☐ Run 6-step system design framework on one problem

    ☐ Discuss conflict resolution strategies (LWW vs 3-way merge)

    ☐ Fakes vs mocks — when to use each

    ☐ Certificate pinning + Play Integrity layered defense

    Day 6 — DSA + Extended Q&A

    Goal: Pattern recognition and legacy Android depth.

    BlockTimeActivity
    Read60 min[20_dsa_solutions.md](20_dsa_solutions.md) — focus on patterns table + study order
    Code90 minSolve on paper (no IDE): Two Sum, LRU Cache, Number of Islands, Course Schedule, Validate BST
    Read60 min[17_amit_shekhar_questions_answers.kt](17_amit_shekhar_questions_answers.kt) — focus RecyclerView (6–7), tooling (16), Java (18); skim rest for extra angles
    Review30 minlearn.html — Topic 20 (DSA patterns)

    Checklist:

    ☐ Can write binary search template from memory

    ☐ Explain LRU Cache: HashMap + doubly linked list

    ☐ Topological sort (Kahn's algorithm) on whiteboard

    ☐ RecyclerView ViewHolder + DiffUtil — explain without notes

    Day 7 — Googleyness + Full Mock + Review

    Goal: Behavioral readiness and final pass.

    BlockTimeActivity
    Read60 min[18_googleyness_and_leadership.md](18_googleyness_and_leadership.md), skim [19_googleyness_questions.md](19_googleyness_questions.md) categories
    Practice90 minSpeak 6 STAR stories aloud (2–3 min each); mock Tier 3 Q20–Q22
    Review60 min[print-guide.html](print-guide.html) — skim weak areas; learn.html — all topics marked "revisit"
    Prep30 minPrepare "Why Google?" with specific product/team; pack questions for interviewer

    Checklist:

    ☐ 6–8 STAR stories written with metrics

    ☐ One failure story and one disagreement story ready

    ☐ Practiced 5 random questions from [19_googleyness_questions.md](19_googleyness_questions.md)

    ☐ Full pass through [16_interview_questions.md](16_interview_questions.md) rapid-fire section

    Interview Week (Day Before Each Round)

    RoundLast-minute review
    Phone screen[20_dsa_solutions.md](20_dsa_solutions.md) patterns 1–14 + [01_android_fundamentals.kt](01_android_fundamentals.kt)
    Domain[16_interview_questions.md](16_interview_questions.md) Tier 1 + [learn.html](learn.html)
    Coding[20_dsa_solutions.md](20_dsa_solutions.md) hard problems 25–27
    System design[15_system_design_android.kt](15_system_design_android.kt) framework + one design
    Googleyness[18_googleyness_and_leadership.md](18_googleyness_and_leadership.md) exemplars + story bank

    Optional Extensions (Week 2+)

    ☐ [13_modern_android.kt](13_modern_android.kt) — KMP, Credential Manager, foldables

    ☐ [14_design_patterns_kotlin.kt](14_design_patterns_kotlin.kt) — GoF + Kotlin advanced

    ☐ Add 5 more DSA problems to [20_dsa_solutions.md](20_dsa_solutions.md)

    ☐ Regenerate [print-guide.html](print-guide.html): python3 scripts/build_print_guide.py

    Good luck. Depth beats breadth — one story told well beats five shallow ones.

    Readme

    README.md · Markdown guide

    Google Android Interview Prep — SDE-2 (4+ YOE)

    > Single source of truth for Google Android SDE-2 (L4) interview preparation.

    > Technical depth, mock Q&A, behavioral prep, and DSA — all in one repo.

    Quick Start

  • 1. Follow the 7-day plan: [STUDY_PLAN.md](STUDY_PLAN.md)
  • 2. Domain prep: Read files 01–17 (start with Critical topics below)
  • 3. Flashcard review: Open [learn.html](learn.html) in a browser
  • 4. Print / offline: Open [print-guide.html](print-guide.html) or regenerate with python3 scripts/build_print_guide.py
  • 5. Behavioral: [18_googleyness_and_leadership.md](18_googleyness_and_leadership.md) + [19_googleyness_questions.md](19_googleyness_questions.md)
  • 6. Coding round: [20_dsa_solutions.md](20_dsa_solutions.md) (27 problems)

    • Interview Format

    Round 1: Phone Screen      — DSA + basic Android
Round 2: Domain-Specific   — Deep Android knowledge (files 01–17)
Round 3: Coding Round      — Algorithm problem-solving (file 20)
Round 4: System Design     — Android system design (file 15)
Round 5: Googleyness       — Behavioral / leadership (files 18–19)

    Study Guide

    Technical — Android Domain (Kotlin)

    Each .kt file is self-contained: concepts, code snippets, interview tips (💡), gotchas (⚠️), and follow-up questions (❓).

    Build Scripts

    Regenerate artifacts after editing source files:

    # Regenerate printable HTML guide after editing source files
python3 scripts/build_print_guide.py

    Optional: scripts/trim_amit_shekhar.py stubs duplicate sections in file 17 — not recommended if you use the full Amit Shekhar reference for prep.

    How to Use

  • 1. Start with [STUDY_PLAN.md](STUDY_PLAN.md) — structured 7-day path
  • 2. Critical topics first (🔴) — appear in almost every Google Android interview
  • 3. Practice out loud — [16_interview_questions.md](16_interview_questions.md) for mock answers
  • 4. Build 6–8 STAR stories — [18_googleyness_and_leadership.md](18_googleyness_and_leadership.md)
  • 5. Review with flashcards — [learn.html](learn.html) between sessions
  • 6. Code on paper — [20_dsa_solutions.md](20_dsa_solutions.md) patterns before coding rounds

    • SDE-2 Expectations (L4)

  • Depth over breadth — explain why, not just how
  • Production experience — discuss trade-offs from real projects
  • Performance awareness — think at Google scale (500M+ DAU)
  • Modern stack — Compose, Coroutines, Hilt, Room, WorkManager
  • Testing mindset — testable architecture is non-negotiable

    • Repo Structure

    01–15   Android technical topics (.kt)
16      Domain round mock Q&A (.md)
17      Extended Q&A — full Amit Shekhar reference (.kt)
18–19   Googleyness prep (.md)
20      DSA — 27 problems (.md)
STUDY_PLAN.md      7-day schedule
learn.html         Interactive flashcards
print-guide.html   Full printable guide (generated)
scripts/           build_print_guide.py (trim_amit_shekhar.py optional)
README.md          Start here

    Code snippets are reference material, not a Gradle project. Copy patterns into your own projects or use them for whiteboard discussion.
